Walking out to Highgate, “talking all the time”﻿ ﻿… they could never have a minute to realise anything at all; rushing along saying things that covered everything and never stopping to realise, talking *about* people and things and never being or knowing anything, and perpetually coming to the blank emptiness of *Highgate*﻿ ﻿… their unconsciousness of everything made them the right sort of people to have the trouble of living in Highgate. They probably walked about with knapsacks on Sunday. But to them even the real country could not be country. All “circles” must be like that in some way; doing things by agreement. The men talking confidently about them, *completely* ignorant of any sort of reality.﻿ ﻿… She came out of her musings when they turned into the Euston Road and ironically watched the men keeping up their talk across the continual breaking up of the group by passing pedestrians. *You’ll* have to walk *back* she interrupted, suddenly turning to Mr. Bowdoin; the buses will have stopped. I never ride in omnibuses frowned Mr. Bowdoin. I shall be back by two.﻿ ﻿… Miriam waited a moment inside the door at Tansley Street listening for silence. The evening fell away from her with the departing footsteps of the two men. She opened the door upon the high quiet empty blue-lit street and moved out into a tranquil immensity. It was everywhere. Into her consciousness of the unpredictable incidents of tomorrow’s Wimpole Street day, over the sure excitement of Eve’s arrival in the evening flowed the light-footed leaping sense of a day new begun, an inexhaustible blissfulness, everything melted away into it. It seemed to smite her, calling for some spoken acknowledgment of its presence, alive and real in the heart of the London darkness. It was not her fault that Eve was not coming to stay at Tansley Street. It came out of the way life arranged itself as long as you did not try to interfere. Roaming along in the twilight she lost consciousness of everything but the passage of dark silent buildings, the drawing away under her feet of the varying flags of the pavement, the waxing and waning along the pavement of the streams of lamplight, the distant murmuring tide of sound passing through her from wide thoroughfares, the gradual approach of a thoroughfare, the rising of the murmuring tide to a happy symphony of recognisable noises, the sudden glare of yellow shop-light under her feet, the wide black road, the joy of the need for the understanding sweeping glance from right to left as she moved across it, the sense of being swept across in an easy curve drawn by the kindly calculable swing of the traffic, of being a permitted cooperating part of the traffic, the coming of the friendly curb and the strip of yellow pavement, carrying her on again into the lamplit greyness leading along to Donizetti’s.

===Chinese===
大家似乎在同一刻起身要走，飘到街上时又似乎都顺路。米丽亚姆发觉自己走在法灵登路上，一边是鲍登先生，一边是另外两个男人里矮的那个，一心盼着独处，盼能慢慢地、自由地逛过她伦敦夜地图上新添的这一块。原来同波希米亚人在一起，晚会结束了也结束不了，还要续上一段强行结伴的步行回家。高个子男人和两位女士在前头大踏步地走，她只得加快脚步，好跟上同伴们那个显然要把他们保持在视野内的意图。说不定到了路顶大家就散了。我们送亨德森小姐到家，然后我同你们接着走到海格特。走到*海格特*——米丽亚姆几乎停住脚，喊了出来。你们今晚要走去海格特（Highgate）？两人都笑了。哦是的，鲍登先生说，那不算什么。*海格特*。光是想想它那北面的遥远，就像对伦敦的一桩冒犯。难怪她同这些人处得像个陌生人。夜里步行去海格特，第二天早晨照常起床。多壮美、多硬朗的一件事。可怕。走着去海格特，「一路说着话」……他们永远没有一分钟去领会任何东西；一路奔忙，说着把一切都盖住的话，从不停下来领会，谈论*着*人和事，却从不成为什么、知道什么，永永远远走向*海格特*空茫茫的空白……他们对一切的无知无觉，正使他们配上海格特居住的这份苦。星期天他们多半背着背包到处走。可对他们，连真正的乡间也算不得乡间。一切「圈子」想必都这样；凡事经商量而行。男人们谈起这些来充满自信，对任何一种真实*彻底*无知。……拐进尤斯顿路时，她从冥想里出来，讥诮地看着两个男人在行人一次次冲散队伍的当口还把谈话续下去。*您*可得走*回去*呢，她突然转向鲍登先生插话；公共马车要收班了。我从不坐公共马车，鲍登先生皱眉道。我两点前就回来了。……米丽亚姆在坦斯利街的门内立了片刻，听那一片静。那个夜晚随着两个男人的脚步声远去，从她身上褪落。她推门面对那条高高的、安静的、空荡的蓝光大街，走进一片安详的浩瀚。它无处不在。流进她对明天温波尔街一天不可预料的种种事故的意识里，流过晚间伊芙到达的那份稳稳的兴奋，流过那轻捷跳跃的、新始的一天的感觉，一种不竭的至福，一切都消融进它里面。它仿佛叩击着她，要她说出一句对它的临在的承认，它活生生、真切切，在伦敦黑暗的心脏里。伊芙不来坦斯利街住，不是她的过错。这是生活自己安顿出来的路数，只要你不去强行干涉。她在薄暮里信步，意识里只剩：一幢幢暗而无声的楼房的移过，脚底下一方方各异的人行石板的退去，沿人行道一注注灯光的涨落，从宽阔的通衢穿她而过的、远远的低语音潮，一条通衢的渐渐临近，音潮涨成一支由认得出的声响合成的快乐交响，脚下骤然亮起的一片店铺的黄光，宽阔的黑色马路，以及过路时那阵需要理解的、横扫的顾盼从右到左的欢喜，那种被车流善意可测的摆动画成一道从容的弧线、整个儿送上对岸的感觉，那种成为车流里被容许的、协作的一分子的感觉，然后是友好地迎上来的路缘石和那条黄色的人行道，重新载上她，走进那片一路通向多尼采蒂餐馆的、灯亮的灰蒙蒙里。

## VI / VI

===Original===
Miriam came forward seeing nothing but the golden gaslight pouring over the white tablecloth. She sat down near Mrs. Bailey within the edge of its radiance. The depths of the light still held unchanged the welcome that had been there when she had come in and found Emile laying the table. There was no change and no disappointment. The smeary mirrors and unpolished furniture were bright in the gaslight, showing distances of interior and gleaming passages of light. In the spaces between the pictures the walls sent back sheeny reflections of the glow on the table. People coming in one by one saying good evening in different intonations and sitting down sending out waves of enquiry, left her undisturbed. There were five or six forms about the table besides Sissie sitting at the far end opposite her mother. They made sudden statements about the weather one after the other. They were waiting to have their daily experience of the meal changed by something she might do or say. Emile was handing round plates of soup. Presently they would all be talking and would have forgotten her. Then she could see them all one by one and get away unseen, having had dinner only with Mrs. Bailey. Mrs. Bailey was standing up carving the joint. When the sounds she made were all that was to be heard, she responded to the last remark about the weather or asked some fresh question about it as if no one had spoken at all. When she was not speaking every movement of her battle with the joint expressed her triumphant affectionate sense of Miriam’s presence. She had made no introductions. She was saying secretly there you are young lady. I told you so. Now you’re in your right place. It’s quite easy you see. The joint was already partly distributed. Emile was handing *three* piled dishes of vegetables. A generous plateful of well-browned meat and gravy appeared before Miriam with Mrs. Bailey’s strong small toil-disfigured hand firmly grasping its edge. She took it to pass it on. Everything was hurrying on.﻿ … That’s yorce my child said Mrs. Bailey. The low murmur was audible round the silent table. Asserting her independence with a sullen formality Miriam thanked her and looked about for condiments without raising her eyes to the range of those other eyes, all taking photographs now that she was forced into movements. Mrs. Bailey placed a cruet near her plate. Yorce she pondered getting angrily away into thought. Mrs. Bailey *could* not know that it might be said to be more correct than yourz. It was an affectation. She had picked it up somewhere from one of those people who carefully say off-ten instead of awfen and it gave her satisfaction to use it, linked rebukingly up with the complacent motherly patronage of which she had boasted to the whole table. The first of Emile’s dishes appeared over her left shoulder and she saw as she turned unprepared, raised heads turned towards her end of the table. She scooped her vegetables quickly and clumsily out of the dishes. In her awkward movements and her unprotected raised face she felt, and felt all the observers seeing, the marks of her disgrace. They saw her looking like Eve nervously helping herself to vegetables in the horrible stony cold dark restaurant of the hostel. They saw that she resented Mrs. Bailey’s public familiarity and could do nothing. She tried to look bored and murmured thank you when she had taken her third vegetable. It sounded out like a proclamation in the intense silence and she turned angrily to her plate trying to remember whether she had heard anyone else thank Emile for vegetables.﻿ … After all she was paying for the meal and her politeness to Emile was her own affair. Abroad people bowed or raised their hats going in and out of shops and said Monsieur to policemen. Her efforts to eat abstractedly and to appear plunged in thought made her feel more and more like a poor relation. The details of her meeting with Eve kept appearing in and out of her attempt to get back her sense of Mrs. Bailey’s house as a secret warmth and brightness added to the many resources of her life. Mrs. Bailey knew that her house had been transformed by the meeting with Eve and was trying to tell her that she was not as independent as she thought.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆朝前走去，眼里只有那倾泻在白桌布上的金色煤气灯光。她在贝利太太近旁落座，坐在那片光辉的边缘之内。光的深处仍旧原封不动地留存着那份迎候——她进门撞见埃米尔（Emile）摆台布时便在的那份迎候。没有变化，也没有失望。污痕斑斑的镜子和未经擦拭的家具在煤气灯下亮堂起来，显出层层的纵深和一道道流光的通道。画与画之间的墙面上，映回桌面上光晕的莹莹反光。人们一个个进来，用种种不同的声调道晚安，坐下，散发出一阵阵打探的波浪，都没有搅扰她。桌边除了坐在尽头、她母亲对面的西西，还有五六个人影。他们接二连三地突然就天气发表断言。他们在等着，等她可能做的什么、说的什么，来把他们每日一餐的体验变一变。埃米尔正端着汤盘轮番上桌。待会儿他们就都会说起话来，把她忘掉。那时她就能把他们一个一个看个分明，再趁人不备抽身走掉，就当只同贝利太太一个人吃了这顿饭。贝利太太正站着切那盘烤肉。当只听得见她弄出的声响时，她就回应最后那句关于天气的话，或者就天气提出个新鲜问题，仿佛方才根本没有人开过口。她不说话的时候，她同那盘肉搏斗的每一个动作，都表露着她对米丽亚姆在场的得意而慈爱的意识。她没有作介绍。她心里暗暗说着：你来了，小姐。我说什么来着。这下你回到你该在的位置了。你看，一点也不难吧。肉已经分掉了一部分。埃米尔正端上*三*盘堆得冒尖的蔬菜。满满一盘烤得焦黄、浇着肉汁的肉出现在米丽亚姆面前，贝利太太强壮的、被劳作磨糙的小手牢牢扣着盘边。她接过来往下传。一切都在匆匆向前。……“这是你的（yorce）了，我的孩子。”贝利太太说。这句低低的絮语在沉默的餐桌四周听得见。米丽亚姆阴沉而拘礼地道了声谢，以此宣示她的独立，一面低头找调料瓶，不肯抬眼去迎接那一排别的眼睛——如今她被迫动作起来，那些眼睛全都在拍照。贝利太太把一套调味瓶放到她盘子旁边。Yorce。她思忖着，愤然遁入思绪深处。贝利太太不可能知道，比起“yourz”，这个音没准儿还算更标准些。这是一种做派。她是从什么人那儿捡来的——那种刻意把 often 念成“奥夫-滕”（off-ten）而不肯念“奥芬”（awfen）的人——用起它来她就称心，同她向全桌夸耀过的那份自得的、母亲式的庇护教人不痛快地连在了一处。埃米尔的第一盘菜在她左肩上方出现，她毫无防备地一回头，看见一颗颗昂起的头转向她这一端。她把蔬菜从盘里又快又笨地拨到自己盘中。在她笨拙的动作和她毫无遮拦仰起的脸上，她感到、并且感到所有旁观者都看见了，她丢脸的印痕。他们看见她像伊芙那样，在宿舍那可怕、阴冷、黑暗的餐馆里紧张兮兮地给自己拨蔬菜。他们看见她对贝利太太当众的亲昵心怀怨愤却又无可奈何。她努力装出厌倦的样子，夹到第三样蔬菜时咕哝了一声谢谢。这一声在凝重的寂静里响得像一道公告，她愤然转向自己的盘子，努力回想有没有听见别的谁为蔬菜谢过埃米尔。……说到底，这顿饭是她付钱的，她对埃米尔客气不客气是她自己的事。在国外，人们进出店铺要躬身或举帽，还要对警察说一声 Monsieur（先生）。她那心不在焉地吃、装作沉浸在思绪里的种种努力，让她越来越觉得自己像个寄人篱下的穷亲戚。她同伊芙会面的种种细节，不断出没于她的努力——努力找回她之于贝利太太这幢房子的感觉：那是添进她生活诸多资粮里的一份隐秘的暖与亮。贝利太太知道她的房子因了同伊芙的会面而变了样，正想告诉她：她并不像自己以为的那样独立。

===Original===
What were the exact things she had told Mrs. Bailey? She had talked excitedly and scrappily and all the time Mrs. Bailey had been gathering information and drawing her own conclusions about the Hendersons. Mrs. Bailey saw Eve’s arrival at the station and her weary resentment of having everything done for her in the London manner, her revenge in the cab, sitting back and making the little abstracted patronising sounds in response to everything that was said to her, taking no interest, and at last saying *how* you run on. She saw something of the hostel.﻿ …

Where’s Mr. Mendizzable? demanded Sissie.﻿ … The Girls’ Friendly; that was the name of that other thing. But that was for servants. The Young Women’s Bible Association was the worst disgrace that could happen to a gentlewoman.﻿ … Eve had liked it. She had suddenly begun going about with an interested revived face eagerly doing what she was told. She was there now, it was her only home, and she must have all her meals there for cheapness; there would be no outside life for her. Her life was imprisoned by those women, consciously goody conscientious servants with flat caps, dominating everything, revelling in the goody atmosphere; the young women in the sitting-room all looking *raw*, as if they washed very early in the morning in cold water and did their shabby hair with cold hands; the superintendent, the watchful official expression on her large well-fed elderly high-school-girl face, the *way* she sat on a footstool with her arms round her knees pretending to be easy and jolly while she recited that it was a privilege and a joy for sisters to be so near to each other﻿ … as if she were daring us to deny it. I shan’t see very much of Eve. She won’t want me to. She will strike up a friendship with one of those young women.﻿ … Miriam found herself glancing up the table towards the centre of a conflict. They were all joined in conflict over some common theme. No one was outside it; the whole table was in an uproar of voices and laughter.﻿ … It was nothing but Miss Scott saying things about Mr. Mendizabal and everyone watching and throwing in remarks.﻿ … Miss Scott was neighing across the table at something that had been said and was preparing to speak again without breaking into her laughter. All faces were turned her way. “What’s that Mr. Joe-anzen says?” laughed Mrs. Bailey towards the last speaker. The invisible man opposite Miss Scott was not even Mr. Helsing; only the younger fainter Norwegian, and this side of him an *extraordinary* person﻿ … an abruptly bulging coarse fringe, a coarse-grained cheek bulging from under an almost invisible deep-sunken eye, and abruptly shelving bust under a coarse serge bodice.

===Chinese===
她到底把哪些事情原原本本告诉了贝利太太？她讲得又兴奋又零碎，而自始至终，贝利太太一直在收集情报，对亨德森家得出她自己的结论。贝利太太看见了伊芙在车站的到来，看见她那疲惫的愠怒——事事都被人按伦敦的路子抢着代劳；看见她在马车里的报复，身子往后一靠，对跟她说的每一句话只回以淡淡几声心不在焉、居高临下的鼻音，毫无兴趣，末了来上一句：你们*可*真能说啊。她还看见了那家宿舍的一点影子。……

“门迪扎布尔先生（Mendizzable，把门迪萨尔的姓念走了样）哪儿去啦？”西西质问道。……女青年友谊会（Girls' Friendly）；那才是那另一处地方的名字。可那是给仆人去的。女子圣经协会（Young Women's Bible Association）则是一位良家女子可能遭逢的最惨的体面扫地。……伊芙却喜欢那里。她忽然开始到处走动，一脸兴致勃勃的复活相，让干什么就急急地干什么。她如今就在那里，那是她唯一的家，为省钱她一日三餐都得在那里吃；她不会再有外面的生活。她的生活被那些女人监禁着——些自觉虔诚、恪尽职守的仆人式的女人，戴着扁平的帽子，支配着一切，陶醉在虔诚的氛围里；起居室里的年轻女子一个个看着*生涩*，仿佛清早很早便用冷水洗过脸，用冰凉的手拢过她们寒素的头发；那位干事，一张保养得很好的、老大的、上了年纪的中学生式的脸上挂着警觉的公务神情，她*那样*坐在脚凳上，双臂抱膝，装出闲适快活的样子，一面背诵着：姐妹们住得这样近，实在是一种福气、一种快乐……仿佛在赌我们谁敢否认。我见不着伊芙几面了。她也不会想见我。她会同那些年轻女子中的一个结下友谊。……米丽亚姆发觉自己朝餐桌上首、朝一场冲突的中心望去。他们全体联结在同一场冲突里，为着某个共同的主题。没有人在局外；整张桌子闹成一片人声与笑语。……其实不过是斯科特小姐说着门迪萨尔先生的种种，大家看着、插着话。……斯科特小姐正为方才哪句话朝桌子对面嘶嘶地笑，一面准备再开口，笑意不断。所有的脸都转向她那边。“那位乔-安森（Joe-anzen，把约翰森 Johannsen 的姓念走了样）先生说什么来着？”贝利太太朝刚说过话的人笑着问。斯科特小姐对面那个看不见的人甚至不是赫尔辛先生；只是那个更年轻、更淡薄的挪威人，而他这一边是个*异样*的人……一副蓦然鼓凸的粗硬刘海，一面粗糙的脸颊从一只几乎看不见的、深陷的眼底下鼓出来，一身粗呢裙衫下蓦然斜削出去的胸脯。

===Original===
“Mr. Yo-hanson says Mr. Mendy-zahble like n-gaiety.” Miriam glanced across the table. That was *all*. That little man with an adenoid voice and a narrow sniggering laugh that brought a flush and red spots all over his face, and shiny straight Sunday school hair watered and brushed flat, made up the party. Next to him was only Polly. Then came Miss Scott on Sissie’s left; then Sissie and round the corner the Norwegian. Everyone looked dreadful in the harsh light, secret and secretly hostile to everyone else, unwilling to be there; and even here though there was nothing and no one there was that everlasting conversational fussing and competition.

“Quite right,” hooted the bulky woman in a high pure girlish voice, “I doan blame ’im.”

Miriam turned towards the unexpectedness of her voice and sat helplessly observing. The serge sleeves were too short to cover her heavy red wrists; her pudgy hands held her knife and fork broadside, like salad servers. Her hair was combed flatly up over her large skull and twisted into a tiny screw at the top just behind the bulge of her fringe. Could she possibly be a boarder? She looked of far less consequence even than the Baileys. Her whole person was unconsciously ill at ease, making one feel ashamed.

“Mrs. m-Barrow is another of ’em,” said the little man with his eyebrows raised as he sniggered out the words.

“I *am* Mr. Gunna, I doan believe in go-an abate with a face like a fiddle.”

===Chinese===
“约翰森先生（Yo-hanson）说，门迪-扎布尔先生顶喜欢热闹。”米丽亚姆朝桌子对面扫了一眼。就*这些*了。那个小个子男人，一副腺样体似的嗡嗡嗓音，窄窄的一声嗤笑就能让满脸泛起红晕和红点，一头抹了水、梳得平平的亮直发，主日学校学生的头发——就凑成了这一席。挨着他的是波莉（Polly）。再过去是西西左手边的斯科特小姐；然后是西西，转过拐角是那个挪威人。人人都在刺目的灯光下显得面目可怖，各怀秘密又暗暗敌视着其他所有人，不情愿待在这里；而即便在这里，虽然既没有什么事、也没有什么人，却照旧有那没完没了的寒暄凑趣和争竞。

“说得对嘛，”那胖大的女人用又高又纯的少女嗓门嚷道，“我可不怪他。”

米丽亚姆朝这嗓音的意外处转过身去，无可奈何地坐着打量。粗呢袖子短得盖不住她粗重的红手腕；她那双肥厚的手宽宽地攥着刀叉，像攥着两把色拉用具。她的头发贴着大颅骨平平地向上梳，在头顶、就在那绺鼓凸刘海的后面，拧成小小的一股旋。她真会是个寄宿客吗？她看着比贝利家的人还要无足轻重得多。她整个人不知不觉地局促不安，看得人替她害臊。

“巴罗太太也是他们一伙的。”小个子男人扬着眉毛、嗤笑着把话送出来。

“我就是冈纳先生一路的，我可不信绷着张提琴似的苦脸到处走。”

===Original===
Mr. Gunner’s laughter flung back his head and sat him upright and brought him back to lean over his plate shaking noiselessly with his head sunk sideways between his raised shoulders as if he were dodging a blow. The eyes he turned maliciously towards Mrs. Barrow were a hard opaque pale blue. His lips turned outwards as he ate and his knife and fork had an upward tilt when at rest. Some of his spots were along the margin of his lips, altering their shape and making them look angry and sore. The eating part of his face was sullen and angry, not touched by the laughter that drew his eyebrows up and wrinkled his bent forehead and sounded only as a little click in his throat at each breath.

“There’s plenty of glum folks abate,” scolded Mrs. Barrow.

Miriam was aware that she was recoiling visibly, and tried to fix her attention on her meal. Mrs. Bailey was carving large second helpings and Emile’s vegetable dishes had been refilled. None of these people thought it extraordinary that there should be all this good meal and a waiter, every day﻿ … it would be shameful to come again for the sake of the meal, feeling hostile. Besides, it would soon be unendurable; they would be aware of criticisms and would resent them. The only way to be able to come would be to pretend to laugh at remarks about people and join in discussions on opinions about cheerfulness and seriousness and winter and summer. They would not know that one was not sincere. They were perfectly sincere in their laughter and talk. They all had some sort of common understanding, even when they disagreed. It was the same everlasting problem again, the way people took everything for granted. They would be pleased, would turn and like one if one could say heartily *isn’t* he a funny little man, mts, my *word*, or well I don’t see anything particularly funny about him, or *oh*, give me the *summer*. But if one did that one would presently be worn and strained with lying, left with an empty excitement, while they went serenely on their way, and the reality that was there when one first sat down with them would have gone. Always and always in the end there was nothing but to be alone. And yet it needed people in the world to make the reality when one was alone. Perhaps just these uninterfering people, when one had forgotten their personal peculiarities and had only the consciousness of them in the distance.﻿ … One might perhaps then wonder sometimes longingly what they were saying about the weather. But to be *obliged* to meet them daily.﻿ … She chided herself for the scathing glance she threw at the unconscious guests. Gunner was smiling sideways down the table again prepared to execute his laugh when he should have caught an eye and sent his grin home. Miriam almost prayed that nothing should provoke him again to speech. During a short silence she cleared her throat elaborately to cover the sound of his eating. Several voices broke out together, but Mrs. Bailey was suddenly saying something privately to her. She raised her head towards the bright promise and was aware of Mr. Gunner thoughtful and serene. There was a pleasant intelligence somewhere about his forehead. If only she could think his head clear and cool and not have to hear again the hot dull hollow resonance of his voice how joyfully she would be listening to Mrs. Bailey. I’ve got a very special message for you young lady she had said and now went on with her eye on the conflict at the end of the table into which Mr. Gunner was throwing comments and exclamations from afar. The room beamed softly in its golden light. From the heart of the golden light Mrs. Bailey was hurrying towards her with good tidings.

“*Hah.*”﻿ …

Mrs. Bailey looked round cloaking her vexation in a bridling smile as Mr. Mendizabal came in sturdily beaming. He sat down amidst the general outcry and Emile busied himself to lay him a place. He shouted answers to everyone, sitting with his elbows on the table. Putting her elbows on the table Mrs. Bailey applauded with little outbursts of laughter. She had dropped the idea of delivering her message. Miriam finished her pudding hurriedly. The din was increasing. No one was aware of her. Cautiously rising she asked Mrs. Bailey to excuse her. You go Miss? shouted Mr. Mendizabal suddenly looking her way. He looked extraordinary, not himself.

===Chinese===
冈纳先生的笑把他的头向后一抛，让他坐得笔直，又把他带回来俯在盘子上，无声地抖着，头侧陷在耸起的双肩之间，像在躲一记打击。他朝巴罗太太恶意转去的眼睛是硬而不透明的淡蓝色。他吃东西时嘴唇向外翻，刀叉停下来时翘着一个向上的斜度。他的几粒红斑就沿着嘴唇的边缘，改变了唇形，让嘴唇看着又愤怒又破疼。他脸上负责吃东西的那一部分阴沉而愤怒，不曾被笑意碰到——那笑只是吊起他的眉毛、让他低俯的额头起了皱，只在每一下呼吸里响成喉咙中一声轻轻的咯哒。

“阴沉脸的人有的是。”巴罗太太数落道。

米丽亚姆意识到自己正在明显地往后缩，便努力把注意力固定在饭食上。贝利太太正切着大份的第二轮，埃米尔的菜盘又添满了。这些人没有一个觉得天天有这样一顿好饭、有一个侍者算得上不寻常……怀着敌意再来吃这顿饭就未免可耻了。再说，很快就将不堪忍受；他们会察觉出批评，会怀恨。要想还能来，唯一的办法就是假装被那些议论人的话逗笑，参加那些关于快活与严肃、冬天与夏天的意见的讨论。他们不会知道一个人并不真心。他们的笑与谈是完全真心的。他们全都有某种共同的理解，哪怕意见相左的时候。又是那个没完没了的老问题——人们把一切视作理所当然。只要能衷心地说上一句：他*多*逗啊，这个小个子男人，啧啧，我的*天*；或者说：嗐，我看他有什么特别好笑的；或者说：*哦*，还是给我*夏天*吧——他们就会高兴，会转过身来喜欢一个人。可要是真那么做了，人很快就会被撒谎磨得疲惫紧绷，只剩一场空洞的兴奋，而他们径自安然走他们的路，刚坐下时还在的那种真实便没有了。到头来永远永远，只剩下独自一人这一条路。然而，独处时的那份真实，偏偏又需要世上有人存在。也许恰恰是这些不来干涉的人——当人忘掉了他们各自的怪相，只远远地存着对他们的意识。……那时人也许偶尔会渴念地想，不知他们正说着天气的什么。可是*不得不*天天同他们见面。……她责备自己朝这些毫无察觉的客人投去的刻毒一瞥。冈纳又朝桌子那头侧过脸去微笑，准备好一旦捕到哪只眼睛、把他的龇牙咧嘴送抵目的地，就把他那套笑执行出来。米丽亚姆几乎要祈祷，千万别再有什么招得他开口。趁一阵短短的静默，她煞有介事地清了清嗓子，盖过他吃东西的声音。几个声音一齐迸发，可贝利太太忽然私下对她说起了什么。她朝那明亮的许诺抬起头，同时察觉冈纳先生正沉静而安详。他额头的什么地方有一种讨人喜欢的灵慧。倘若她能认定他头脑清爽冷静、不必再听见他那火热、沉闷、空洞的共鸣的嗓音，她该多么欢欣地听贝利太太说下去。我有一句顶要紧的话要告诉您呢，小姐，她方才说过，此刻接着说，眼睛望着桌子那端的冲突，冈纳先生正从远处朝那里投去评论和惊叹。房间在金色的光里柔柔地发亮。从那金色光焰的心脏里，贝利太太正带着好消息朝她赶来。

“*哈。*”……

贝利太太环顾四周，把恼怒裹进一抹昂然的笑里，这时门迪萨尔先生精神抖擞、满面生光地走了进来。他在一片鼎沸的人声中落座，埃米尔忙着为他摆位。他双肘撑在桌上，大声回答着每一个人。贝利太太也把双肘撑上桌面，迸出一阵阵小笑为他喝彩。她已经打消了传话的念头。米丽亚姆匆匆吃完布丁。喧声愈涨。没有谁留意她。她小心地起身，请贝利太太原谅她先走一步。您要走吗，小姐？门迪萨尔先生忽然朝她这边望来，大声喊道。他看上去异样，不像他自己。

---

===Original===
Eve’s shop was a west-end blaze of flowers. The window was blocked with flowers in jars, tied up in large bundles. In front were gilt baskets of hothouse flowers. Propped in the middle were a large flower anchor and a flower horseshoe, both trimmed with large bows of white satin ribbon﻿—women in white satin evening dresses with trains, bowing from platforms﻿—on either side were tight dance buttonholes pinned on to heart-shaped velvet mounts.

It was strange to be able to go in.﻿ … Going in to see an employee was not the right way to go into a west-end shop.﻿ … There *was* Eve; standing badly in a droopy black dress on a bare wet wooden floor. Cut flowers in stone jam pots, masses of greenery lying on a wet table. Hulloh aren’t your *feet* wet demanded Miriam irritably. Eve started and turned, looking. She was exhausted and excited, grappling dreamily with abrupt instructions with a conservatory smell competing with them; trying to become part of a clever arrangement to collect the conservatory smell for sale. She stepped slenderly forward; all her old Eve manner, but determined to guard against disturbance; making sounds without speaking, and the faint shape of a tired smile. She was worn out with the fatigue of trying to make herself into something else, but liking it and determined not to be reminded of other things. Even her hair seemed to be changed. Full of pictures of Eve, gracefully dressed and with piled brown hair Miriam’s eyes passed in fury over the skimpy untidy sham shop-assistant, beginning a failure defensively, *imagining* behind it that she was taking hold of London.﻿ … Won’t you catch *cold*? You get used to it mouthed Eve nervously turning her head away and waiting, fumbling a scattered spray of smilax. Eve had always loved smilax. Did it seem the same to her now? Fancy *you* said Miriam, in all this damp. They were both miserable and Eve was not going to put it right. All her strength and interest was for this new thing. Do you like it? said Miriam beginning again. Yes awfully flushed Eve looking as if she were going to cry. It was too late. I suppose its awfully interesting asked Miriam formally, opening a conversation with a stranger. Mps said Eve warmly I simply love it. It makes you frightfully tired at first, but I find I can do things I never dreamed I could. I don’t *mind* standing in the wet a bit now. You have to if you’re obliged to. Eve was *liking* hardness imposed by other people. *Liking* the prices of her new life. Accepting them without resentment. People would despise and like her for that. Perhaps she would succeed in staying on if her strength did not give way. Her graceful dresses and leisurely brown hair going further and further away.﻿ … Do you *serve*? Ssh. I’m learning to. Eve would not look, and wanted her to be gone. I’m free for lunch said Miriam snappily, holding to the disappearing glory of her first coming out into London in the middle of a weekday. Eve should have guessed and stopped being anything but Eve being taken out to lunch. We could go to an A.B.C. Oh I can’t come *out* murmured Eve ignoringly.

===Chinese===
伊芙的铺子是西区的一片花的焰火。橱窗里塞满了瓶插的花、扎成大捆的花。前面是一篮篮镀金篮里的温室花卉。当中支着一只大大的花圈锚和一只花马蹄铁，都缀着大大的白缎蝴蝶结——穿拖着裙裾的白缎晚礼服的女人，在讲台上躬身致意——两侧是紧紧实实的舞会纽孔花，别在心形天鹅绒托座上。

居然就能这样走进去，真奇怪。……为了看一个雇员而走进一家西区的铺子，不是进西区铺子的正当方式。……伊芙*果然*在那里；一身松垂的黑裙站得歪歪的，站在裸露潮湿的木地板上。石果酱瓶里的切花，大片大片的绿叶摊在湿漉漉的台面上。喂，你的*脚*不湿吗？米丽亚姆烦躁地质问。伊芙一惊，转过身来，望着。她疲惫而亢奋，恍恍惚惚地同突如其来的指令角力，一股温室的气味同那些指令较着劲；她正努力变成一套聪明布置的一部分，好把温室的气味收集起来出售。她纤细地跨前一步；还是她全套旧日的伊芙做派，只是打定了主意提防搅扰；不出声地做着口型，一个倦笑的淡淡轮廓。她被“把自己变成别的东西”的劳顿弄得精疲力竭，却喜欢这样，并且打定主意不许人提起别的。连她的头发都像是变了。满脑子都是伊芙的身影——衣着优雅、棕色头发高高堆起——米丽亚姆的眼睛狂怒地扫过这个寒酸邋遢的冒牌店员，一个防备中的、正在开场的失败，在这一切背后*想象*着自己抓住了伦敦。……你不会*着凉*吗？慢慢就习惯了。伊芙无声地做出这话的口型，紧张地把头扭开，等着，摆弄一枝散落的菝葜藤（smilax）。伊芙从前最爱菝葜藤。如今在她眼里它还是一样的吗？真想不到是*你*，米丽亚姆说，泡在这么大的潮气里。她们两个都苦兮兮的，而伊芙不打算把这一层修补好。她全部的气力和兴致都在这件新事物上。你喜欢这里吗？米丽亚姆重新开口。喜欢，喜欢极了。伊芙涨红着脸说，看上去就要哭出来。已经太迟了。我想这工作一定非常有意思吧。米丽亚姆拘拘谨谨地问，同一个陌生人攀谈。嗯哼。伊芙热切地应着，我简直爱死它了。一开始累得要命，可我发现我能做从前做梦也想不到的事。如今站在湿地上我一点也*不介意*了。不得已的话，也就只好如此。伊芙正在*喜欢*别人强加的辛苦。*喜欢*她新生活的种种代价。毫无怨愤地接受它们。人们会因此看轻她、也喜欢她。倘若她的气力撑得住，也许她真能留下去。她优雅的裙衫和从容的棕发，越走越远。……你*接待*顾客吗？嘘。我正在学。伊芙不肯抬眼，只盼她走。我午饭有空。米丽亚姆急急地说，紧紧抓住那份正在消逝的光荣——她头一回在一个工作日的半晌午走进伦敦。伊芙本该猜到，本该放下一切别的身份，只做那个被人带出去吃午饭的伊芙。我们可以去 ABC 茶馆（A.B.C.）。哦，我可不能*出来*。伊芙恍若无闻地低声说。

===Original===
Miriam ordered another cup of coffee and went on reading. There was plenty of time. Eve would not appear at Tansley Street until half-past. In looking up at the clock she had become aware of detailed people grouped at tables. She plunged back into Norway, reading on and on. Each line was wonderful; but all in a darkness. Presently on some turned page something would shine out and make a meaning. It went on and on. It seemed to be going towards something. But there was nothing that *anyone* could imagine, nothing in life or in the world that could make it clear from the beginning, or bring it to an end. If the man died the author might stop. Finis. But it would not make any difference to anything. She turned the pages backwards rereading passages here and there. She could not remember having read them. Looking forward to portions of the dialogue towards the end of the book she found them familiar; as if she had read them before﻿ … she read them intently. They had more meaning read like that, without knowing to what they were supposed to refer. They were the *same*, read alone in scraps, as the early parts. It was all one book in some way, not through the thoughts, or the story, but something in the author. People who talked about the book probably understood the strange thoughts and the puzzling hinting story that began and came to an end and left everything as it was before. The author did not seem to suggest that you should be sorry. He seemed to know that at the end everything was as before, with the mountains all round.﻿ … The electric lights flashed out all over the A.B.C. at once.﻿ … Miriam remained bent low over her book. Only you had been in Norway, in a cottage up amongst the mountains and out in the open. She read a scene at random and another and began again and read the first scene through and then the last. It was all the *same*. You might as well begin at the end.﻿ … In *Norway*, up among the misty mountains, in farms and cottages looking down on fjords with glorious scenery about them all the time are people, sitting in the winter by fires and worrying about right and wrong. They *wonder* but more gravely and clearly than we do. Torrents thunder in their ears and they can see mountains all the time even when they are indoors. “Ibsen’s Brand” is about all those worrying things, in magnificent scenery. You are *in* Norway while you read. That is why people read books by geniuses and look faraway when they talk about them. They know they have been somewhere you cannot go without reading the book.﻿ … *Brand*. You are in the strangeness of Norway﻿—and then there are people saying things that might be said anywhere. But with something going in and out of the words all the time. Ibsen’s genius. You can’t understand it or see where it is. Each sentence looks so ordinary, making you wonder what it is all about. But taking you somewhere, to stay, forgetting everything, until it is finished. An hour ago Ibsen was just a name people said in a particular way, a difficult wonderful mystery, and *improper*. Why do people say he is improper? He is exactly like everyone else, thinking and worrying about the same things. But putting them down in a background that is more real than people or thoughts. The life in the background is in the people. He does not know this. Why did he write it? A book by a genius is alive. That is why “Ibsen” is superior to novels; because it is not quite about the people or the thoughts. There is something else; a sort of lively freshness all over even the saddest parts, preventing your feeling sorry for the people. Everyone ought to know. It ought to be on the omnibuses and in the menu. All these people fussing about not knowing of Ibsen’s Brand. A volume, bound in a cover. Alive. Precious. What *is* Genius? Something that can take you into Norway in an A.B.C.

She wandered out into Oxford Street. There was a vast fresh gold-lit sky somewhere behind the twilight. Why did Ibsen sit down in Norway and write plays? Why did people say Ibsen as if it were the answer to something? Walking along Oxford Street with a read volume of Ibsen held against you is walking along with something precious between two covers which makes you know you are rich and free.﻿ … She wandered on and on in an expansion of everything that passed into her mind out and out towards a centre in Norway. She wondered whether Ibsen were still alive. A vast beautiful Norway and a man writing his thoughts in a made-up play. Genius. People go about saying Ibsen’s Brand as if it were the *answer* to *something* and Ibsen knows no more than anyone else.﻿ … She arrived at Tansley Street as from a great distance, suddenly wondering about her relationship with the sound of carts and near footfalls. Mrs. Bailey was standing in the doorway seeing someone off. *Eve.* Forgotten. I couldn’t get here before; I’m *so* sorry. Mrs. Bailey had disappeared. Eve stepped back into the hall and stood serenely glowing in the half-light. Are you going? I must, in a minute. Eve was looking sweet; slenderly beautiful and with her crimson-rose bloom; shy and indulgent and unenviously admiring as she had been at home; and Mrs. Bailey had been having it all. Can’t you come upstayers? Not this time; I’ll come again some time. Well; you must just tell me; wot you been doing? Talking to Mrs. Bailey? Yes. Eve had been flirting with Mrs. Bailey; perhaps talking about religion. Isn’t she funny? I *like* her; she’s perfectly *genuine*, she means what she says and really *likes* people. Yes; I know. Isn’t it funny? I don’t think it’s funny; it’s very beautiful and rare. Would you like to be here always? Yes; I could be always with Mrs. Bailey. Every day of your life forever and ever? *Rather.* Yes; I know. And y’know there are all *sorts* of interesting people. I wish you lived here Eve. Eve glanced down wisely smiling and moved slenderly towards the door. What about Sunday? Couldn’t you come round for a long time? No breathed Eve restrainingly, I’m going to *Sallies*. All Eve’s plans were people. She moved, painfully, through things, from person to person.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆又要了一杯咖啡，接着读。时间充裕。伊芙要到半点之后才会到坦斯利街。抬头看钟的当儿，她才留意到一桌桌聚坐着的人，细节分明。她又一头扎回挪威，一页一页读下去。每一行都妙极了；却全都处在一片黑暗里。待会儿在哪一页翻过去的纸上，会有什么东西亮出来，生出一个意思。它一路向前。仿佛正走向什么。可世上没有任何*人*能想象出那是什么，生命里、世界上没有什么东西能让它从头讲清，或者给它一个收束。要是那个男人死了，作者也许就停笔了。终。可那对什么也不会有丝毫改变。她把书页往回翻，东一段西一段地重读。她记不起自己读过这些。翻到书末尾前的几段对话，她觉得眼熟；仿佛从前读过……她专注地读它们。这样读——不知道它们本该对应什么——反倒更有意味。单拎出碎片来读，它们同开头的部分是*一样*的。不知怎么，这整本是一部书，不靠那些思想，也不靠那个故事，而是靠作者身上的什么东西。谈论这本书的人，多半领会了那些古怪的思想和那个费解的、影影绰绰的故事——故事开了头又收了尾，把一切留在原地。作者似乎并不暗示你该为之难过。他仿佛知道，到末了一切照旧，四面群山环抱。……电灯霎时间在整间 ABC 茶馆里齐齐亮起。……米丽亚姆依旧低低地伏在她的书上。只不过你到过挪威，到过群山之上的一座农舍，到过旷野之中。她随手读了一幕，又读一幕，再从头来，把第一幕读完，然后读最后一幕。全是*一样的*。从结尾读起也未尝不可。……在*挪威*，在雾蒙蒙的群山之上，在俯瞰着峡湾的农场和农舍里，四周永远是最壮丽的风景，坐着些人，冬夜里围在炉边，为对与错忧心。他们也*追问*，却比我们追问得更庄重、更清明。急流在他们耳边轰鸣，他们时时刻刻看得见群山，哪怕人在屋里。易卜生的《布朗德》（Ibsen's Brand）写的正是那些忧心的事，衬在壮丽的风景里。读的时候，你人就在*挪威*。所以人们读天才的书，谈起它们时眼神悠远。他们知道自己去过一个地方，不读那本书，你就去不成。……《布朗德》。你置身于挪威的奇境——然后有些人说着随处都说得出的话。可是有什么东西在字里行间进进出出，一刻不停。易卜生的天才。你无法理解它，也看不见它在哪儿。每个句子都显得那么平常，让你纳闷这到底都说的是什么。却把你带去一个地方，留下不走，忘掉一切，直到读完。一个钟头以前，易卜生还只是个名字，人们用一种特别的方式说起它，一个艰深而奇妙的谜，而且*不体面*。为什么人们说他不体面？他同所有人一模一样，想着、忧着同样的事。只是把它们放进一个比人物和思想更真实的背景里。背景里的生命就在人物身上。他自己并不知道这一点。他为什么要写它？天才的书是活的。所以“易卜生”比小说高出一等；因为它并不全然是关于那些人物或那些思想的。还有别的东西；一种活泼的新鲜劲儿遍布全书，连最悲伤的部分也不例外，拦住你，不让你为人物难过。人人都应该知道。它应该印上公共马车、印上菜单。所有这些人忙忙碌碌，不知道易卜生的《布朗德》。一卷书，装在一个封面里。活的。珍贵。天才*到底*是什么？是能把你带进挪威的什么东西——在一间 ABC 茶馆里。

她游荡出来，到了牛津街（Oxford Street）。暮色背后某处，有一片浩大而清新的金色亮天。易卜生为什么要坐在挪威写剧本？为什么人们说“易卜生”，仿佛它是某个问题的答案？把一卷读过的易卜生贴身挟着走在牛津街上，就是挟着两片封面之间某种珍贵的东西走着，它让你知道你富足而自由。……她游荡着，游荡着，心头掠过的一切都在舒张，向外、再向外，伸向挪威的一个中心。她想知道易卜生是否还在世。一片广袤美丽的挪威，一个男人把他的思想写进一个虚构的剧本。天才。人们四处说着“易卜生的《布朗德》”，仿佛它是*某样东西*的*答案*，而易卜生自己同任何人一样一无所知。……她抵达坦斯利街时仿佛来自很远的地方，忽然琢磨起自己同手推车声和近处脚步声的关系。贝利太太正站在门口送客。*伊芙。*被忘掉的。我来不及早些到；真是*太*对不住了。贝利太太已经不见了。伊芙退回门厅，在半明半暗里宁静地放着光。你要走了吗？我得走了，马上。伊芙看上去甜美；纤细地美丽，带着她玫瑰红的青春光艳；羞怯、纵容、不带嫉妒地欣赏着人，一如在家里的样子；而这一切全都让贝利太太一个人受用了。你不上楼来坐坐？这回不啦；我改天再来。唉；你可得跟我说说；你都做了些啥呀？跟贝利太太说话来着？是啊。伊芙一直在同贝利太太逗趣寒暄；也许还在谈宗教。她逗不逗？我*喜欢*她；她*地道*极了，她说话算话，是真的*喜欢*人。是啊；我知道。怪不怪？我可不觉得怪；这非常美，也非常难得。你愿意一直住在这儿吗？愿意；我能一直跟贝利太太在一起。一辈子天天如此，永远永远？*那当然。*是啊；我知道。你不知道，这儿有各*种各*样有意思的人。真希望你住在这儿，伊芙。伊芙明智地含笑垂下眼，纤细地朝门口移去。星期天怎么样？你不能来待上大半天？不行。伊芙克制地轻声说，我要去*萨利家*（Sallies，救世军的招待所）。伊芙的计划全是人。她艰难地在事物中间穿行，从一个人到另一个人。

---

===Original===
Dr. Hurd held the door wide for Miriam to pass out and again his fresh closely knit worn brick-red face was deeply curved by the ironically chuckling hilarious smile with which he had met the incidents of the “awful German language.” That of the *fatherland*, the happy *fatherland*, nearly *dislocates* my *jaw* she could imagine him heartily and badly singing with a group of Canadian students. She smiled back at him without saying anything, rapidly piecing together the world that provoked his inclusive deeply carved smiles; himself, the marvellous little old country he found himself in as an incident of the business of forcing himself to be a doctor, his luck in securing an accomplished young English lady to prepare him for the struggle with the great medical world of Germany; his triumphant chuckling satisfaction in getting in first before the other fellows with an engagement to take her out.﻿ … The grandeur of this best bedroom of Mrs. Bailey was nothing to him. The room was just a tent in his wanderings.﻿ … For the moment he was going to take a young lady to a concert. That was how he saw it. He was a simple boyish red-haired open extension of Dr. von Heber. When she found herself out in the large grime and gloom of the twilit landing she realised that he had lifted her far further than Dr. von Heber into Canada; he was probably more Canadian. The ancient gloom of the house was nothing to him, he would get nothing of the quality of England in his personal life there, only passing glimpses from statements in books and in the conversation of other people. He did not see her as part of it all in the way Dr. von Heber had done talking at the table that night and wanting to talk to her because she was part of it. He saw her as an accomplished young lady, but a young lady like a Canadian young lady and a fellow was a fool if he did not arrange to take her out quick before the other fellows. But there was nothing in it but just that triumph. “I’ll get a silk hat before Sunday”; he would prepare for her to go all the way down to the Albert Hall as a young lady being taken to a concert; the Albert Hall on Sunday was brass bands; he thought they were a concert. His world was thin and open; but the swift sunlit decision and freedom of his innocent reception of her in his bedroom lifted the dingy brown house of her long memories into a new background. She was to be fêted, in an assumed character and whether she liked it or no. The four strange men in the little back sitting-room were her competing friends, the friends of all nice young ladies. He was the one who had laughed the laugh she had heard in the hall, of course. They never appeared but somehow they had got to know of her and had their curious baseless set ways of thinking and talking about her. Being doctors and still students they ought to be the most hateful and awful kind of men in relation to women, thinking and believing all the horrors of medical science; the hundred golden rules of gynæcology; if they had been Englishmen they would have gone about making one want to murder them; but they did not; Dr. Hurd was studying gyn’kahl’jy, but he did not apply its ugly lies to life; to Canadians women were people﻿ … but they were all the *same* people to Dr. Hurd.

===Chinese===
赫德医生（Dr. Hurd）为米丽亚姆把门拉得大开，让她出去，他那张清新的、咬合紧密的、磨损的砖红色脸又一次被那含讥带诼、呵呵哈哈的笑深深弯出弧度——凭这笑，他迎接了“可怕的德语”（the awful German language）的种种事变。*祖国*的德语、快乐的*祖国*的德语，差点儿把我的*下巴*都*卸*下来——她能想象他同一群加拿大学生一起，起劲而蹩脚地唱着这支歌。她朝他笑笑，什么也没说，飞快地拼合起那个引出他这包容一切的、刻着深沟的笑的世界：他自己；他发现自己置身其中的这个奇妙的小小古国，只是他硬逼自己当医生这桩事业里的一段插曲；他的好运，聘到一位多才多艺的英国年轻小姐为他备考，迎战德国堂堂的医学界；他抢在别的伙计前头先下手为快、订下带她出游的那份得意的、窃笑的满足。……贝利太太这间上好卧室的气派，在他眼里什么也不是。这房间不过是他漫游途中的一顶帐篷。……眼下，他要带一位年轻小姐去听音乐会。他是这么看的。他是冯·黑贝尔医生（Dr. von Heber）的一根单纯、孩子气、红头发、敞亮的开枝散叶。当她发觉自己已置身暮色楼梯平台那大片污黑与幽暗里，她意识到他把她比冯·黑贝尔医生托举得更高、更深地进了加拿大；他多半更像加拿大人。这房子古老的幽暗对他毫无分量，他在那边的人生活里得不到英格兰的质地，只有书里的陈述和旁人谈话里惊鸿一瞥。他不曾像冯·黑贝尔医生那晚在餐桌上那样把她看作这一切的一部分、因为她是其中一部分而想同她谈话。他把她看作一位多才多艺的年轻小姐，但一位同加拿大年轻小姐一样的年轻小姐，而哪个伙计要是不赶紧抢在别人前头安排带她出去，那就是傻子。可这里面除了那份得胜，再无别的。“星期天以前我要去弄一顶丝质礼帽”；他要备好让她一路去到阿尔伯特音乐厅（Albert Hall），做一位被带去听音乐会的年轻小姐；星期天的阿尔伯特音乐厅是铜管乐队；他以为那就是音乐会。他的世界单薄而开阔；但他在自己卧室里接待她时那种阳光般迅捷的决断与自由，把她那载着长长记忆的、褐蒙蒙的旧房子托进了一个新的背景。她将要受一番礼遇，以一个假定的身份，不管她乐不乐意。小后客厅里那四个陌生男人是她竞相献殷勤的朋友，一切体面年轻小姐的朋友。他当然就是她在门厅里听见那一声笑的笑主。他们从不露面，可不知怎么已经得知了她，对她有他们那套古怪的、无根无据的想法定式和谈法定式。他们是医生又还是学生，论对女人的态度本该是顶可恨、顶可怕的那种男人，想着、信着医学科学的种种骇人；妇科学的百条金科玉律；他们若是英国人，早就招得人想杀了他们；可他们不；赫德医生正在攻读妇科学（gynæcology），却不把那门学问丑陋的谎言应用到生活里；在加拿大人看来，女人是人……可在赫德医生看来，她们也全都是*同样的人*。

---

===Original===
That evening both Dr. Heber and Dr. Hurd appeared at dinner. Mrs. Bailey tumultuously arranged them opposite each other to her right and left. Miriam could not believe they were going to stay until they sat down. She retreated to the far end of the table taking her place on Sissie’s right hand, separated from Dr. von Heber by the thin Norwegian and the protruding bulk of Mrs. Barrow. Mr. Mendizabal with a pencil and paper at the side of his plate was squarely opposite to her. His *méfiant* sallies to the accompaniment of Sissie’s giggles and Miss Strong’s rapid sarcastic remarks, made a tumult hiding her silence. She heard nothing of the various conversations sprouting easily all round the table. The doctors were far-off strongholds of serenity, unconscious of their serenity, unconscious of her and of their extraordinary taking of the Baileys and Mr. Gunner for granted.﻿ … Dr. von Heber was a silence broken by small courteously curving remarks. Dr. Hurd laughed his leaping delighted laugh in and out of an unmeditated interchange with Mr. Gunner and Mrs. Bailey. If she had been at their end of the table they would not have perceived her thoughts, but they would have felt her general awareness and got up at last disliking her. They changed the atmosphere but could not make her forget the underlying unchanged elements nor rid her of her resentment of their unconsciousness of them. There was a long interval before the puddings appeared. Mrs. Bailey was trying to answer questions about books. Dr. Hurd did not care for reading, but liked to be read to, by his sisters, in the evening, and had come away, at the most exciting part of a book﻿ … a wonderful authoress, what’s her name now﻿—﻿Rosie﻿—﻿Newchet.﻿ … He was just longing to know how it ended. Was it sweet and wonderful, or too dreadful for anything to contemplate a student, a fully qualified doctor having Rosa Nouchette Carey read to him by his sisters? Dr. von Heber was not joining in. Did he read novels and like them? No one had anything to say; no one here knew even of Rosa Nouchette Carey﻿ … and that man Hunter﻿ … he’s great﻿ … he’s father’s favourite; what’s this, Mr. Barnes of New York.﻿ … Archibald Clavering Gunter said Miriam suddenly, longing to be at the other end of the table. Beg pardon? said Sissie turning aside for a moment from watching Mr. Mendizabal’s busy pencil. There he is shouted Mr. Mendizabal flinging out his piece of paper﻿—gastric ulcer﻿—there he is. There was a drawing of a sort of crab with huge claws.﻿—My beautiful gastric ulcer﻿—Have you been to the ’ospital today Mr. Mendizzable asked Mrs. Bailey through the general laughter. I have been madame and I come away. They say they welcome me inside again soon. *Je m’en fiche.* The faces of both doctors were turned enquiringly. Dr. Hurd’s look of quizzical sympathy passed on towards Miriam and became a mask of suppressed hysterical laughter. Perhaps he and Dr. Heber would scream and yell together afterwards and make a great story of a man in a London pension. Dr. Hurd would call him a *cure*. My word isn’t that chap a *cure*? Brave little man. Caring for nothing. How could he possibly have a gastric ulcer and look so hard and happy and strong. *What* was Dr. von Heber silently thinking? The doctors disappeared as soon as dinner was over, Dr. von Heber gravely rounding the door with some quiet formal phrases of politeness, and the group about the table broke up. He’s a bit pompous Mr. Gunner was saying presently to someone from the hearthrug. Was he daring to speak of Dr. von Heber? Presently there were only the women left in the room. Miriam felt unable to depart and hung about until the table was cleared and sat down under the gas protected by her notebook. The room was very quiet. Sissie and Mrs. Bailey were mending near a lamp at the far end of the table. Miriam’s thoughts left her suddenly. The tide of life had swept away leaving an undisturbed stillness, a space swept clear. She was empty and nothing. In all the clamour that had passed she had no part. In all the noise that lay ahead, no part. Strong people came and went and never ceased, coming and going and acting ceaselessly, coming and going, and here, at her centre, was nothing, lifeless thoughtless nothingness. The four men studied apart in the little room, away from the empty lifeless nothingness﻿ … the door opened quietly. Mrs. Bailey and Sissie looked expectantly up and were silent. Something had come into the room. Something real, clearing away the tumult and compelling peaceful silence. She exerted all her force to remain still and apparently engrossed, as Dr. von Heber placed an open notebook and a large volume on the table exactly opposite to where she sat and sat down. He did not see that she was astonished at his coming nor her still deeper astonishment in the discovery of her unconscious certainty that he would come. A haunting familiar sense of unreality possessed her. Once more she was part of a novel; it was right, true like a book, for Dr. Heber to come in in defiance of everyone, bringing his studies into the public room in order to sit down quietly opposite this fair young English girl. He saw her apparently gravely studious and felt he could “pursue his own studies” all the better for her presence. She began writing at random, assuming as far as possible the characteristics he was reading into her appearance. If only it were true; but there was not in the whole world the thing he thought he saw. Perhaps if he remained steadily like that in her life she could grow into some semblance of his steady reverent observation. He did not miss any movement or change of expression. Perhaps you need to be treated as an object of romantic veneration before you can become one. Perhaps in Canada there were old-fashioned women who *were* objects of romantic veneration all their lives, living all the time as if they were Maud or some other woman from Tennyson. It *was* glorious to have a real, simple homage coming from a man who was no simpleton, coming simple, strong and kindly from Canada to put you in a shrine.﻿ … I have always liked those old-fashioned stories because I have always known they were *true*. They have lived on in Canada. Canadian men have kept something that Englishmen are losing. She turned the pages of her notebook and came upon the scrap crossed through by Mr. Mendizabal. She read the words through forcing them to accept a superficial meaning. Disturbance about ideas would destroy the perfect serenity that was demanded of her. Be good sweet maid and let who will be clever. Easy enough if one were perpetually sustained by a strong and adoring hand. Perhaps more difficult really to be good than to be clever. Perhaps there were things in this strong man that were not perfectly good and serene. He exacted his own serenity by sheer force; that was why he worshipped and looked for natural serenity.﻿ … Presently she stirred from her engrossment and looked across at him as if only just aware of his presence. He did not meet her look but a light came on his face and he raised his head and turned towards the light to aid her observation. The things that are beginning to be called silly futile romances are true. Here *is* the strong silent man who does not want to talk and grin.﻿ … He would love laughter. Freed from worries and sustained by him one could laugh all one’s laughter out and dance and sing through life to a happy sunsetting.﻿ … Was he *religious*? She found she had risen to her feet with decision and began collecting her papers in confusion as if she had suddenly made a great clamour. Dr. Heber rose at once and with some quiet murmuring remark went away from the room. Miriam felt she must get into the open and go far on and on and on. Going upstairs through the house and into her room for her outdoor things she found her own secret belongings more her own. In the life she shyly glanced at, out away somewhere in the bright blaze of Canadian sunshine her own secret belongings would be more her own. That was one of the secrets of the sheltered life﻿ … one of the things behind the smiles of the sheltered women; their own secret certainties intensified because they were surrounded; perhaps in Canada men respected the secret certainties of women which they could never share. With your feet on that firm ground what would it matter how life went on and on? There was someone in the hall. Mr. Mendizabal in a funny little short overcoat.

“You go out Miss?” he said cheerfully.

“I’m going for a walk,” she said eagerly, her eyes on the clear grey and black of the hat he was taking from the hall stand.

“I too go for a walk” he murmured cramming the soft hat on to his resisting hair and opening the door for her.

===Chinese===
当晚，黑贝尔医生和赫德医生双双出现在晚餐桌上。贝利太太风风火火地把他们安排在自己左右两侧、彼此相对。直到他们落座，米丽亚姆才相信他们真会留下来。她退到桌子远端，坐在西西的右手边，同冯·黑贝尔医生之间隔着瘦挪威人和巴罗太太凸出的庞大身躯。门迪萨尔先生盘子边搁着纸笔，方方正正地坐在她正对面。他那些*疑神疑鬼*（méfiant）的俏皮出击，配上西西的咯咯笑和斯特朗小姐（Miss Strong）连珠的挖苦，掀起一阵鼎沸，把她的沉默盖了个严实。餐桌四周轻松萌发的种种谈话，她一句也没听见。两位医生是远处的安详的堡垒，对自己的安详毫无知觉，对她毫无知觉，对他们把贝利一家和冈纳先生视作理所当然的那份异乎寻常也毫无知觉。……冯·黑贝尔医生是一段静默，不时被小小的、恭谨地打着弯的话语打断。赫德医生那跳跃的欢畅笑声，在同冈纳先生和贝利太太一场不假思索的你来我往中进进出出。倘若她坐在他们那一头，他们察觉不出她的思想，但他们会感觉到她那份总体的警觉，末了会起身走开，心里腻烦她。他们改变了气氛，却不能让她忘掉底下那些不曾改变的东西，也除不掉她对“他们对此毫无知觉”的怨愤。布丁上来之前有长长一段空当。贝利太太正努力回答关于书的问题。赫德医生不爱读书，但喜欢晚上由他的姐妹们读给他听，而且人走的时候，正读到一本书*顶*要紧的关头……一位了不起的女作家，她叫什么来着——罗西——纽切特。……他正一心想知道结局如何。一位学生、一位满资格的医生，让姐妹们读罗莎·努谢特·凯里（Rosa Nouchette Carey）给他听——这又甜美又奇妙呢，还是可怕得让人没法细想？冯·黑贝尔医生没有加入。他读小说吗？喜欢吗？谁都没话可说；这里没有人哪怕听说过罗莎·努谢特·凯里……还有那个亨特……他了不起……他是家父的心头好；这是什么，《纽约的巴恩斯先生》（Mr. Barnes of New York）。……阿奇博尔德·克莱弗林·冈特（Archibald Clavering Gunter）。米丽亚姆突然说，一心渴望坐到桌子那一头去。您说什么？西西说，一时从门迪萨尔先生忙碌的铅笔上转过脸来。他在这儿呢！门迪萨尔先生喊着，甩出他那张纸片——胃溃疡——他在这儿呢。纸上画着一只长着巨螯的、螃蟹似的玩意儿。——我美丽的胃溃疡——您今天去医院了吗，门迪扎布尔先生？贝利太太隔着满堂大笑问。去过了，夫人，我就回来了。他们说我很快就能再住进去啦。*我才不在乎呢*（Je m'en fiche）。两位医生的脸都探询地转了过来。赫德医生那副打趣的同情目光移向米丽亚姆，变成一副强忍歇斯底里狂笑的面具。也许他和黑贝尔医生回头要一起又叫又嚷，把一个伦敦膳宿公寓里的男人编成一大篇故事。赫德医生会管他叫个*活宝*。我的天，这家伙是不是个*活宝*？勇敢的小个子男人。什么都不在乎。他怎么可能得了胃溃疡，还看上去这样硬朗、快活、强壮。冯·黑贝尔医生心里*究竟*在想什么？晚饭一结束两位医生就消失了，冯·黑贝尔医生庄重地绕过门口，说了几句安静的客套话，桌边的一群人散了。他有点儿摆架子。冈纳先生不一会儿在炉边地毯上对什么人说。他竟敢说冯·黑贝尔医生？片刻后屋里只剩下女人们。米丽亚姆觉得自己走不了，磨蹭到桌子收拾干净，在煤气灯下坐下来，拿她的笔记本作掩护。房间很静。西西和贝利太太在桌子远端一盏灯旁做针线。米丽亚姆的思绪忽然离她而去。生命的潮水扫荡而过，留下一片不受惊扰的静止，一方扫净了的空间。她空了，什么也不是。方才过去的一切喧腾里没有她的份。前方横着的一切喧嚣里，也没有份。强壮的人们来来去去从不停歇，来去、行动，无休无止，来来去去，而这里，在她的中心，是虚无，无生命无思想的虚无。四个男人在小房间里各自用功，离开这空洞的、无生命的虚无……门轻轻开了。贝利太太和西西期待地抬头，随即哑然。有什么东西进了屋。某种真实的东西，扫清喧腾，迫出安宁的寂静。她竭尽全力保持静止、显出一副专心致志的样子，这时冯·黑贝尔医生把一册摊开的笔记本和一部大书放在桌上，正对她坐的地方，坐了下来。他看不出他的到来让她惊讶，也看不出她更深一层的惊讶——发现自己竟一直无意识地确信他会来。一种缠人的、熟悉的非真之感攫住了她。她又一次成了一部小说的一部分；黑贝尔医生无视众人走进来，把他的功课带进这间公共起居室，好安静地坐在这位白皙的英国年轻姑娘对面——这样才对，像书里一样真实。他看她表面上一派庄重好学，觉得因为她在场，他可以更好地“从事他自己的研究”。她开始随意写起来，尽量扮演出他从她的外表上读进去的那些特性。要是真的就好了；可全世界都没有他以为他看见的那样东西。也许，倘若他就这样稳稳地留在她的生活里，她能长出几分配得上他那凝定而虔敬的注视的模样。他不放过任何一个动作、任何一丝表情变化。也许，人需要先被当作浪漫崇敬的对象对待，然后才能成为那样的对象。也许在加拿大，有一些旧式的女人，一辈子*就是*浪漫崇敬的对象，一直活得仿佛自己是《莫德》（Maud），或者丁尼生（Tennyson）笔下的别的什么女人。得到一份真实的、单纯的崇敬，来自一个并非笨蛋的男人，单纯、强健、和善地从加拿大来把你供进神龛——这*确是*荣耀。……我一直喜欢那些旧式的故事，因为我一直知道它们是*真的*。它们在加拿大活了下来。加拿大的男人保存了英国男人正在失去的某种东西。她翻动笔记本，翻到被门迪萨尔先生划掉的那一角。她把那些字读了一遍，强令它们接受一个表面的意思。为思想而起的纷扰，会毁掉她被要求的完美安详。做个好姑娘，谁聪明就让他聪明去。若有一只强壮而爱慕的手时时托着，这何难之有。也许，做个好人实在比做个聪明人更难。也许这个强壮的男人身上，也有并非全然良善、全然安详的东西。他自己的安详是凭蛮力强征来的；正因如此，他才崇拜、才寻找天然的安详。……不久她从专注中动了动，朝他望过去，仿佛这才刚刚察觉他的存在。他没有迎她的目光，可他脸上亮起一点光，他抬起头，转向灯光，方便她的观察。那些开始被人叫作愚蠢无聊的传奇小说的东西，是真的。这*不就是*那个不想说话、不想龇牙咧嘴的强壮沉默的男人么。……他会热爱笑声。免于忧患、又得他扶持，人可以把一生的笑都笑尽，跳着舞、唱着歌过完一生，直到一个幸福的日落。……他*信教*吗？她发觉自己已决然站起身，慌乱地收拢纸张，仿佛她方才突然弄出了一场大喧哗。黑贝尔医生立刻起身，低声说了句什么，离开了房间。米丽亚姆觉得必须到户外去，走远些，再远些，再远些。上楼穿过房子、回房间取外出衣物时，她发觉自己那些隐秘的物件更属于她自己了。在她羞怯地瞥见的那种生活里——在远处什么地方、加拿大阳光明晃晃的烈焰里——她那些隐秘的物件会更属于她自己。那是受庇护的生活的秘密之一……是受庇护的女人们笑容背后的东西之一；她们自己隐秘的确信，因为四下有人环绕而愈发强烈；也许在加拿大，男人们尊崇女人那些他们永远无法分享的隐秘的确信。脚踩在那样的实地上，日子再怎么向前延展又有什么要紧？门厅里有人。门迪萨尔先生，穿着一件好笑的小短大衣。

“您出去走走吗，小姐？”他喜洋洋地说。

“我去散散步。”她急切地说，眼睛盯着他从衣帽架上取下的那顶帽子清晰的灰黑二色。

“我也去散散步。”他低声说，把软帽子扣上他不肯服帖的头发，为她开了门。

---

===Original===
This was one of those *mild* February days; it is a mistake to imagine that the winter is gone; but it is gone in your mind; you can see ahead two summers and only one winter. I go with you was meant as a question.﻿ … It was on the tip of her tongue to turn and say you should have said shall I go with you; she was rebuked by a glimpse of Mr. Mendizabal swinging sturdily unconsciously along on the gutter side of the narrow width of pavement, swinging his stick, the strong modelling of his white face unconscious under his strong black hair and the jaunty sweep of his black banded grey hat. “Jaunty and debonair”; but without a touch of weakness. What a lovely *mild* evening; extraordinary for the time of year; he would be furious at being interrupted for that, thinking of her as a stiff formal institutrice and shouting something ironic that would bring the world about their ears. *Quel beau temps;* that was it.

“Quel *beau* temps.” They had reached the Gower Street curb and stood waiting to plunge through the passing traffic.

“*Une soirée superbe mademoiselle*” shouted Mr. Mendizabal in a smooth flattened squeal as they crossed side by side; “hah-*eh*!” he squealed pushing her off to dart clear of a hansom and away to the opposite curb. Miriam pulled back just in time, receiving the angry yell of the driver full in her upturned face. Mr. Mendizabal was waiting unconcernedly outside the chemist’s, singing, with French words. She disposed hastily of the incident, eager to be walking on through the darkness towards the mingled darkness and gold of the coming streets. They went along past the grey heights of University College Hospital, separate creatures of mysteriously different races; she expected that when they reached the light she would find herself alone﻿—and swung with one accord round into the brilliance of the Tottenham Court Road; the tide of light and sound raising them into a companionship that needed no bending into shapes of conversation. It was something to him and it was something to her, and they threaded their way together, meeting and separating and rejoining, unanimous and apart. We are both *batteurs de pavé*, she thought; both people who must be free to be nothing; saying to everything *je m’en fiche*﻿ … the hushed happiness that had begun in the dining-room half an hour ago seized her again suddenly, sending her forward almost on tiptoe. It was securely there; the vista it opened growing in beauty as she walked. There was some source of light within her, something that was ready to spread out all round her and ahead and flow over the past. It confirmed scenes she had read and wondered at and cherished, seeking in vain in the world for women who were like the women described in them. She understood what women in books meant by *sacred* “It is all too sacred for *words*.” There was no choice in all that; only secret and sacred beauty; unity with all women who had felt in the same way; the freedom of following certainties. Outside it was this other self untouched and always new, her old free companion attending to no one. She tossed Mr. Mendizabal shreds of German or French whenever the increasing throng of passing pedestrians allowed them to walk for a moment side by side.

===Chinese===
这是那种*温和*的二月天气中的一天；以为冬天已经过去，那就错了；可在你心里它已经过去；你看得见前方有两个夏天，只有一个冬天。“我同您一起去”本意是个问句。……她话到舌尖，就想转身说：您本该说“我可以同您一起去吗”；她被眼前的一幕止住了——门迪萨尔先生正壮实地、无意识地走在窄窄人行道靠排水沟的一侧，摆着他的手杖，他白脸的强健轮廓在浓黑的头发和黑箍灰帽潇洒的倾侧下毫无知觉。“潇洒而俊逸”；却没有一丝软弱。多可爱的*温和*的夜晚；一年里这个时节可真少见；为这个打断他，他会大为光火，把她想成一位拘谨刻板的家庭女教师（institutrice），喊出什么讥诮话来，闹得满世界在耳边炸开。*Quel beau temps*（多好的天气）；就是这个。

“*Quel beau* temps（多好的天气）。”他们已走到高尔街（Gower Street）的路缘，站着等待冲过川流不息的车辆。

“*Une soirée superbe mademoiselle*（一个绝美的夜晚，小姐）！”门迪萨尔先生用一种圆滑压扁的尖叫喊着，这时他们并肩穿过马路；“哈——*呃*！”他尖叫着把她一推，闪开一辆双轮马车的锋头，奔到对面的路缘。米丽亚姆及时缩回，车夫愤怒的吼叫兜头浇在她仰起的脸上。门迪萨尔先生正毫不在意地等在药房门外，唱着歌，唱着法语词。她把这一幕匆匆处置掉，急着往前走，穿过黑暗，走向前方街道交融的黑与金。他们一路走过大学学院医院（University College Hospital）灰色的高大楼宇，是两个神秘地不同族类的分立造物；她原以为等走到亮处，她会发觉自己独自一人——却同他步调一致地一转身，拐进托特纳姆宫路（Tottenham Court Road）的辉煌；光与声的浪潮把他们托进一种无须弯折成谈话形状的伙伴关系。这于他是点什么，于她也是点什么，他们一起穿行，相遇、分开、再会合，一致而又各自。我们两个都是*马路漫游者*（batteurs de pavé），她想；都是必须自由到可以一无所有的人；对一切说一声*我才不管呢*（je m'en fiche）……半小时前在餐室里开始的那种屏息的幸福，忽然又攫住了她，托着她几乎踮着脚尖向前。它稳稳地在着；它敞开的景致随她走着而愈见其美。她体内有某个光的源头，有一样准备向她四周、向她前方铺展开来、漫过过去的东西。它印证了她读到过、惊叹过、珍藏着的一些场景——她曾在世间徒劳地寻找像那些场景里描写的女人一样的女人。她懂得了书里的女人说*神圣*时是什么意思：“这一切太神圣了，*言语*不足以言之。”那里面没有取舍；只有隐秘而神圣的美；同所有曾同样感受过的女人合而为一；追随确信的自由。在这之外，是这个不曾被触碰、永远常新的另一个自我，她那自由的老伙伴，谁也不理会。每当渐密的行人人流容许他们并肩走上一小会儿，她就朝门迪萨尔先生抛出几片德语或法语。

===Original===
His apparent oblivion of her incoherence gave full freedom to her delight in her collection of idioms and proverbs. Each one flung out with its appropriate emphasis and the right foreign intonation gave her a momentary change of personality. He caught the shreds and returned them woven into phrases increasing her store of convincing foreignness, comfortably, from the innocence of his polyglot experience, requiring no instructive contribution from her, reassuringly assuming her equal knowledge, his conscious response being only to her joyousness, his eyes wide ahead, his features moulded to gaiety. The burden of her personal dinginess and resourcelessness in a strong resourceful world was hidden by him because he was not aware of dinginess and resourcelessness anywhere. Dingy and resourceless she wandered along keeping as long as her scraps of convincing impersonation should hold out, to her equal companionship with his varied experience; bearing within her in secret unfathomable abundance the gift of ideal old-English rose and white gracious adorable womanhood given her by Dr. Heber. At the turning into Oxford Street they lost each other. Miriam wandered in solitude amidst jostling bodies. The exhausted air rang with lifeless strident voices in shoutings and heavy thick flattened unconcerned speech; even from above a weight seemed to press. Clearer space lay ahead; but it was the clear space of Oxford Street and pressed upon her without ray or break. Once it had seemed part of the golden west-end; but Oxford Street was not the west-end. It was more lifeless and hopeless than even the north of London; more endurable because life was near at hand. Oxford Street was like a prison﻿ … the embarrassment of her enterprise came upon her suddenly; the gay going off was at an end; perhaps she might get away and back home alone up a side street. Amidst the shouting of women and the interwoven dark thick growlings of conversations she heard Mr. Mendizabal’s ironic snorting laugh not far behind her. Glancing round from the free space of darkness she had reached she saw him emerge shouldering from a group of women, short and square and upright and gleaming brilliantly with the remains of his laughter. A furious wrath flickered over her. He came forward with his eyes ahead unseeing, nearer, near, safe at her side, *her* little foreign Mr. Mendizabal, mild and homely.

Here is Ruscino’s mademoiselle, *all*ons, we will go to Ruscino *all*ons! Ruscino, in electric lights round the top of the little square portico, like the name of a play round the portico of a theatre, the sentry figure of the commissionaire, the passing glimpse of palm ferns standing in semidarkness just inside the portico, the darkness beyond, suddenly became a place, separate and distinct from the vague confusion of it in her mind with the Oxford Music Hall; offering itself, open before her, claiming to range itself in her experience; open, with her inside and the mysteries of the portico behind﻿ … continental London ahead of her, streaming towards her in mingled odours of continental food and wine, rich intoxicating odours in an air heavy and parched with the flavour of cigars, throbbing with the solid, filmy thrilling swing of music. It was a café! Mr. Mendizabal was evidently a habitué. She could be, by right of her happiness abroad. She was here as a foreigner, all her English friends calling her back from a spectacle she could not witness without contamination. Only Gerald knew the spectacle of Ruscino’s. “Lord, Ruscino’s; *Lord*.”﻿ … In a vast open space of light, set in a circle of balconied gloom, innumerable little tables held groups of people wreathed in a brilliancy of screened light, veiled in mist, clear in sharp spaces of light, clouded by drifting spirals of smoke. They sat down at right angles to each other at a little table under the central height. The confines of the room were invisible. All about them were worldly wicked happy people.

===Chinese===
他对她语无伦次显然的浑然不觉，给了她充分的自由去享受她那批惯用语和谚语的收藏。每一句甩出去，带上恰当的重音和地道的外国腔调，都让她瞬时变换一次人格。他接住那些碎片，把它们织成短语送回来，增厚她那令人信服的异邦感的库存——从容地，凭着他的多语阅历的浑朴天真，不需要她贡献什么指教，令人安心地假定她懂得一样多，他有意识的回应只对着她的欢畅，他的眼睛望着前方开阔处，他的五官塑成快活的模样。她个人的暗淡无光、她在一个强悍而多资的世界里的无依无靠，被他遮住了，因为他根本不知道世上还有暗淡与无依无靠这回事。她就这样暗淡而无依无靠地走着，只要她那些可信的化身碎片还撑得住，就维持着与他那多样阅历的对等伙伴关系；怀里藏着一份幽深莫测的丰盈——黑贝尔医生所赠的那份理想的、古英国玫瑰与白的、优雅可爱的女性气质的礼物。在拐进牛津街的路口，他们走散了。米丽亚姆独自在推推搡搡的人体中间游荡。耗竭了的空气里响着无生气的尖利嗓音，有叫喊，有沉重、厚钝、压扁了的漠然的谈话；连头顶上方都仿佛压着一团重量。前方躺着更清爽的空间；可那是牛津街的清爽空间，无光无隙地朝她压来。它一度仿佛是金色西区的一部分；可牛津街不是西区。它比伦敦北区还要无生气、无希望；只是因为生活近在手边，才更可忍受。牛津街像一座监狱……她此行的窘迫忽然袭上来；那场兴冲冲的出走到头了；也许她该抽身，独自取一条小街回去。在女人们的叫喊和交织的、又黑又浓的谈话低吼中，她听见门迪萨尔先生那含讥带讽的喷鼻的笑，就在她身后不远。从她已抵达的那片自由的黑暗里回头一瞥，她看见他正从一群女人里挤撞着冒出来，矮而方，笔挺，脸上还闪耀着他笑声的余烬。一阵狂怒在她身上掠过。他朝前走来，眼睛望着前方，什么也没看见，近了，更近了，安然到了她身边——*她的*小小的外国人门迪萨尔先生，温厚而家常。

这儿就是鲁西诺了，小姐，*走*吧，我们这就去鲁西诺，*走*吧！鲁西诺（Ruscino's），几个电灯字母绕着小小的方形门廊顶端，像一座剧院门廊顶上绕着的戏名，门卫那哨兵般的人影，门廊内侧半明半暗中伫立的棕榈蕨一瞥而过，再外面是黑暗——它忽然成了一个地方，从她心里同牛津音乐厅（Oxford Music Hall）搅在一起的模糊混沌里分立出来；把自己呈上来，在她面前敞开，要求排进她的阅历；敞开着，她在里面，门廊的诸种神秘在身后……欧陆的伦敦在她前方，裹着欧陆食物与酒的混合气味向她涌来，浓郁的、醉人的气味，在一种被雪茄味熏得滞重焦渴的空气里，随着音乐那沉实、朦胧、悸人的摆荡而搏动。这是一家咖啡馆！门迪萨尔先生显然是位常客。凭她在国外的那份幸福，她也可以是。她以一个外国人的身份到了这里，她所有的英国朋友都在唤她回去——这场面她一看就要被玷污。只有杰拉尔德（Gerald）知道鲁西诺的这场面。“老天，鲁西诺；*老天*。”……在一片浩大的敞亮里，四下环着一圈带廊座的幽暗，数不清的小桌围坐着一群群的人，裹在遮蔽的光的辉亮里，蒙在薄雾里，在锋利的光的间隙里清明，被飘移的烟的螺旋弄浑。他们在一盏中央高灯下的小桌旁成直角坐定。房间的四缘看不见。他们四周全是世俗的、邪恶的、快活的人。

---

===Original===
She could understand a life that spent all its leisure in a café; every day ending in warm brilliance, forgetfulness amongst strangers near and intimate, sharing the freedom and forgetfulness of the everlasting unchanging café, all together in a common life. It was like a sort of dance, everyone coming and going poised and buoyant, separate and free, united in freedom. It was a heaven, a man’s heaven, most of the women were there with men, somehow watchful and dependent, but even they were forced to be free from troublings and fussings whilst they were there﻿ … the wicked cease from troubling and the weary are at rest﻿ … she was there as a man, a free man of the world, a continental, a cosmopolitan, a connoisseur of women. That old man sitting alone with a grey face and an extinguished eye was the end of it, but even now the café held him up; he would come till death came too near to allow him movement. He was horrible, but less horrible than he would be alone in a room; he had to keep the rules and manage to behave; as long as he could come he was still in life.﻿ … White muslin wings on a black straw hat, a well-cut check costume and a carriage, bust forward, an elegant carriage imposing secrets and manners.﻿ … Miriam turned to watch her proceeding with a vague group of people through the central light towards the outer gloom. *Voilà une petite qui est jolie* she remarked judicially.﻿—*Une jeune fille avec ses parents*﻿—rebuked Mr. Mendizabal. Even he, wicked fast little foreigner did not know how utterly meaningless his words were. He was here, in Ruscino’s quite simply. He sat at home, at the height of his happy foreign expansiveness. He had no sense of desperate wickedness. He gave no help to the sense of desperate wickedness; pouring like an inacceptable nimbus from his brilliant strong head was a tiresome homeliness. She flung forth to the music, the shining fronds of distant palm ferns; sipped her liqueur with downcast eyes and thought of an evening along the digue at Ostend, the balmy air, the telescoping brilliant interiors of the villas, the wild arm-linked masquerading stroll, Elsie had really looked like an unprincipled Bruxelloise﻿ … foreigners were all innocent in their depravity.﻿ … To taste the joy of depravity one must be English.﻿ … Hah; Strelinsky! *Ça va bien, hein?* A figure had risen out of the earth at Mr. Mendizabal’s elbow and stood looking down at him; another foreigner. She glanced with an air of proprietorship; a slender man in a thin faded grey overcoat, a sharp greyish yellow profile and small thin head under a dingy grey felt hat. Strelinsky. Mr. Mendizabal stood sturdily up bowing with square outstretched hand, wrapped in the radiating beam of his smile. I present you Mr. Strelinsky. A musician. A composer of music. His social manner was upon him again; fatherliness, strong responsible hardworking kindliness. The face under the grey hat turned slowly towards her. She bowed and looked into eyes set far back in the thin mask of the face. Her eyes passed a question from the expressionless eyes to the motionless expressionless face. How could he be a composer; looking so﻿ … vanishing? Strelinsky﻿ … *Morceau pour piano*﻿ … that must be *he* standing here; did you write this she said abruptly and hummed the beginning. It sounded shapeless and toneless, but there was a little tune just ahead. She broke off short of it not sure that he was attending; the world burst into laughter; his face turned slowly and stopped looking downwards across her, his eyes fixed in a dead repetition of the laughter in which she was drowning. He stood in space in a faded coat and hat, a colourless figure clothed by her feebleness in lively dignity and wisdom﻿ … grouped inaccessibly beyond the vast space were solid tables filled with judges; dim figures stood in judgment in the amber light under the gallery where palms stood; she was drowning alone, surrounded by a distant circle of palms. Eleven. We must go miss stated Mr. Mendizabal cordially. Miriam rose. The tide of café life flowed all round her. She wandered blissfully out through the misty smoke-wreathed golden light, threading her way amongst the tables towards the black and gold of the streets. Far away behind her, staying in the evening, Strelinsky blocked the view, moving, fixed avertedly, with eyes in his shoulders along an endless narrowing distance of café.

===Chinese===
她能理解一种把全部闲暇都消磨在咖啡馆里的生活；每一天都终结于温暖的辉亮，在亲近而贴心的陌生人中间忘却，分享这永不变更的咖啡馆的自由与忘却，大家共守一种共同的生活。它像某种舞蹈，人人来来去去，从容而轻快，分立而自由，在自由中联合。这是一处天堂，男人的天堂，多数女人是陪着男人来的，不知怎么总带着警戒和依附，可就连她们，在这里的时候也被迫从烦扰和琐碎里解脱出来……恶人止息搅扰，困乏人得享安息……她在这里是个男人，一个自由的世界中人，一个欧陆人，一个世界公民，一个鉴赏女人的行家。那个独自坐着的灰脸老人、一只眼睛已经熄灭，就是这一切的尽头；可即便现在，咖啡馆还托着他；他会一直来，直到死亡逼得太近、容不得他动弹。他可怕，但比他独自待在一间屋里要不可怕些；他得守规矩、得像个体面人；只要他还来得了，他就还在生活里。……黑草帽上一对白纱的翅膀，一套剪裁讲究的格子衣裙和一副仪态，胸脯朝前，一副优雅的、强加着秘密与规矩的仪态。……米丽亚姆转过身，看她带着一群影影绰绰的人穿过中央的亮处走向外围的幽暗。*Voilà une petite qui est jolie*（瞧，这个小姑娘多俏）。她评判般地说。——*Une jeune fille avec ses parents*（一个跟着父母的姑娘）——门迪萨尔先生纠正道。连他，这个邪恶、性急的小个子外国人，也不知道他这句话是多么全然地没有意义。他就在这里，在鲁西诺，简简单单。他坐在自己家里，坐在他快活的外国人式的舒张的顶点上。他没有绝望的邪恶感。他对那份绝望的邪恶感毫无助益；从他明亮的强健的头颅上倾泻下来的，像一圈教人无法消受的光晕，是一种恼人的家常气。她朝音乐抛掷过去，朝远处棕榈蕨发亮的叶丛；垂着眼啜她的利口酒，想起奥斯坦德（Ostend）海堤（digue）上的一个傍晚，温软的空气，别墅里一节节伸缩着、亮堂堂的内景，挽着胳膊化装闲逛的狂欢队伍，埃尔西（Elsie）那时看上去真像个没了原则的布鲁塞尔女人（Bruxelloise）……外国人堕起乐来全都天真无邪。……要尝堕落的快乐，人必须是英国人。……哈，施特雷林斯基（Strelinsky）！*Ça va bien, hein?*（还好吗，嗯？）一个人影从门迪萨尔先生的肘边、从地里冒出来，站着俯视他；又一个外国人。她带着几分主人的神气瞥了一眼；一个瘦削的男人，穿一件薄薄的褪色灰大衣，一副瘦锐的灰黄侧脸，一顶污灰毡帽下一颗瘦小单薄的脑袋。施特雷林斯基。门迪萨尔先生壮实地站起来，伸出方正的手掌致意，裹在他微笑四射的光柱里。我给您介绍，施特雷林斯基先生。一位音乐家。一位作曲家。他的社交面孔又回到了他身上；父辈的慈和，强壮、负责、勤恳的善良。灰帽下那张脸缓缓转向她。她欠了欠身，望进一双深深嵌在脸的薄面具后面的眼睛。她的眼睛从那双毫无表情的眼睛递过去一个问号，落到那张纹丝不动的、毫无表情的脸上。他怎么可能是个作曲家；看上去这么……易消散？施特雷林斯基……*Morceau pour piano*（《钢琴小品》）……站在这里的必定就*是*他；这是您写的吗？她突然说，哼出了开头。哼得不成形、不成调，可就在前头有个小小的曲调。她在够到它之前打住了，不能肯定他有没有在听；整个世界哄然大笑；他的脸缓缓转过来，停在她上方偏过一点的地方朝下望着，他的眼睛僵死地重复着她正在其中溺水的那些笑声。他立在虚空里，穿着褪色的大衣和帽子，一个没有颜色的人形，被她的孱弱披上了鲜活的尊严与智慧……在浩渺空间之外、够不着的地方，一群群坐满的是判官的一张张结实的桌子；一些朦胧的人影在廊座下的琥珀色光线里、在棕榈树立着的地方，站着审判；她独自溺着水，被远远一圈棕榈围着。十一点了。我们该走了，小姐。门迪萨尔先生恳切地说。米丽亚姆起身。咖啡馆生活的潮水在她四周流淌。她飘飘然穿出那雾蒙蒙、烟缭绕的金色灯光，在桌间穿行，走向街道的黑与金。远远的身后，留在那个夜晚里的，是施特雷林斯基挡住了视线，移动着，却又僵在一旁的注视里，眼睛长在肩上，沿着一道无尽收窄的咖啡馆的纵深。

## VII / VII

===Original===
Miriam found her old prayerbook and scribbled her name on the flyleaf.﻿ … Bella de Castro writing *from mother* under her name in her bible﻿ … *feeling* something, privately, not knowing that anyone would see it.﻿ … The sunlight pouring in on the thin bible page; the words written plumply with one of Bella’s blunt uncared-for pencils. Her thick ropy black plait, brilliant oily Italian eyes in her long fat handsome face; staring out of the window sullenly waiting for schooldays to be at an end; her handsome horrible brother on horseback; just the same; the high-water marks above her wrists when she washed her hands, and then, from mother, stubbed carefully, *meaning*.

The pencilled *Miriam* gave a false meaning to the prayerbook. There was no indiarubber, she would have to take it down as it was. It was a letter, written to Dr. von Heber, supposed to be written when she was a girl.﻿ … She carried the book downstairs. The Baileys were still sitting by the fire with their backs to Dr. von Heber standing alone in the twilight in the middle of the room. She came forward, handing the book stiffly and sat busily down to the piano again, angrily recording his quiet formal thanks and silent swift departure. She began playing where she had left off; telling Dr. von Heber as he went downstairs that he had come up and made a scene and interrupted her; that her chosen evening was to sit, with the Baileys, playing the piano; that she was not a churchgoer.

He had come so suddenly; after so long; if she had not been so lost in the disappointing evening she would have been ready. If she had not suddenly been so prepared, so rushing forward and feeling after he had spoken as if the words had been long ago and they had been to church together and come back before all the world there would not have been in his voice the reproachful affronted anticipation of her stupidity.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆找出她那本旧祈祷书，在扉页上草草签下自己的名字。……贝拉·德·卡斯特罗（Bella de Castro）在她《圣经》里自己的名字底下写上*母亲赠*……私下里*感受*着什么，不知道会有人看见。……阳光泼洒在薄薄的圣经纸页上；那行字是用贝拉一截秃了头、没人爱惜的铅笔写的，笔画圆圆胖胖。她那条油亮乌黑、绳索似的粗辫子，她那张长而丰腴的俊脸上那双意大利式的、亮得冒油光的眼睛；闷闷地望着窗外，只盼学生生涯早点到头；她那个英俊而可怕的哥哥骑在马上；全都一模一样；还有她洗手时手腕上方那一圈洗不净的高水位污痕；再然后，是“母亲赠”，一笔一画，用心地，*别有所指*。

铅笔写的“米丽亚姆”给这本祈祷书添了一层虚假的意思。没有橡皮，她只好原样拿下去。它是一封信，写给冯·黑贝尔医生的，假托还是姑娘时写下的。……她把书拿了下去。贝利一家还坐在炉火边，背对着独自站在暮色房间中央的冯·黑贝尔医生。她走上前，生硬地把书递过去，又忙碌地坐回钢琴前，愤愤地记下他安静客套的道谢和他无声而迅疾的离去。她从方才停下的地方接着弹；一边告诉他——他正下楼去——说他上楼来大惊小怪了一场、打断了她的兴致；说她的如意夜晚就是同贝利一家坐在这里弹琴；说她不是个上教堂的人。

他来得那么突然；隔了那么久；倘若她没有整个迷失在这个令人失望的夜晚里，她本会有所准备。倘若她不曾突然准备得那么充分、那么急急地迎向前去，在他开口之后感觉仿佛那些话早已说过、仿佛他们早已一同上过教堂又当着全世界回来，他的嗓音里就不会有那种责备的、受了冒犯的、对她愚钝的预期。

---

===Original===
Perhaps he had really suddenly thought downstairs that it would be nice to go to church, not knowing that that was one of the real effects of falling in love﻿ … just thinking in the course of his worldly studies that there was church and he was in himself a churchgoer and ought to go more often and coming up to borrow a prayerbook from the *Baileys*. No. Suddenly in the room, standing in the unknown drawing-room for the first time in his steady urbane confident way, waiting, a little turned towards the piano. The Baileys had neither spoken nor moved; they were afraid of him; but Mrs. Bailey would have made herself say, Well, doctor, to the amazing apparition. They simply waited, held off by his waiting manner. “*I think this is a good evening to go to church.*” What have you been doing all this time? Where do you go, going out so often? What are you doing sitting here playing? We ought to be going to church; we two. Here I am professing churchgoing and idiotically confessing myself come all the way from Canada without a prayerbook and making a pretence of borrowing your prayerbook because we *must* be in church together. Dr. Hurd’s impressions had had no effect upon him.﻿ … But now he had gone back into his own life not only thinking that she was not a churchgoer, but feeling sure that her own private life of coming and going had no thoughts of him in it.

Dr. Hurd sitting on the omnibus with *amusement* carving deep lines on his brick-red face and splintering out of his eyes into the hot afternoon glare; the neat new bowler with the red hair coming down underneath it, the well-cut Montreal clothes on his tough neat figure; immovable, there for the afternoon. Forced to go on and on isolated with the brick-red grin and the splintering green eyes through the afternoon heat, in the midst of a glare of omnibus people, on their way to a brass band in the Albert Hall, thinking they were going to a *concert*. He did not know what made a concert. Sitting with the remains of his grin, waiting for the things he had been taught to admire, unable to find anything without his mother and sisters; missing Canadian ladies with opinions about everything; waiting all the time to be managed in the Canadian women’s way.﻿ … He must have told the others about it afterwards, his face crinkling at them and they listening and agreeing.

===Chinese===
也许他当真只是在楼下突然想到，去趟教堂不错，并不知道那正是堕入情网的真实效验之一……只是在尘世功课的间隙想到有教堂这回事、他自己骨子里是个上教堂的人、该多去去，于是上楼来向*贝利一家*借一本祈祷书。不。是突然出现在房间里，以他那种沉稳、文雅、自信的姿态头一次站进这间陌生的客厅，等待着，微微侧身朝着钢琴。贝利一家既没说话也没动弹；他们怕他；可贝利太太本会逼着自己对这惊人的幽灵说上一句：哎，医生。他们就那么等着，被他那等待的姿态逼开了。“*我想今晚是个上教堂的好晚上。*”这段时间你都在做什么？你时常外出，都去哪儿？你坐在这里弹琴算怎么回事？我们该去教堂；我们两个。我在这里自命信教，又蠢得可以地自认千里迢迢从加拿大来竟没带一本祈祷书，装出借你们祈祷书的样子，因为我们*必须*一同在教堂里。赫德医生的种种印象不曾对他起半点作用。……可如今他已回到他自己的生活里，不仅认定她不是个上教堂的人，还确信她那来来去去的私生活里根本没有他。

赫德医生坐在公共马车上，*快活*在他砖红的脸上刻出深深的纹路，从他眼里迸出碎光，扎进午后灼热的强光里；崭新的圆顶礼帽，红头发从帽檐底下露出来，剪裁讲究的蒙特利尔（Montreal）衣裳穿在他结实齐整的身架上；纹丝不动，整个下午就在那儿。人被迫同这砖红的龇笑和迸碎的绿眼睛一起，孤零零地一路走一路走，穿过午后的暑热，夹在一车亮晃晃的公共马车乘客中间——他们正去阿尔伯特音乐厅听铜管乐队，自以为去听一场*音乐会*。他不知道是什么构成一场音乐会。他带着笑的残余坐着，等着那些人家教他欣赏的东西，离了母亲和姐妹便什么都找不着；想念那些对一切都有见解的加拿大女士；时时刻刻等着被用加拿大女人的法子打点。……事后他准是把这一场讲给了那几个人听，脸朝他们皱起笑纹，他们听着，附和着。

---

===Original===
It had begun the moment after he had suggested the concert. I’ll get a new top hat before then. The awful demand for a jest. His way of waiting as if one were some queer being he was waiting to see say or do something anyone could understand was the same as the English way only more open. But English people like that did not care for music and did not have books read to them. Perhaps his parents belonged to the other sort of English and he had the stamp of it, promising seriousness and a love of beautiful things, and forced by life into the jesting way of worldly people who seemed to have no sacred patches at all. Quick words, bathed in laughter heaped up into a questioning of what the *matter* was. Men, demanding jests and amusement; women succeeding only by jesting satirically about everything.

Von Heber’s a man who’ll *carve* his way.﻿ … My. He’s *great*. Carve his way; one of those phrases that satisfy and worry you; short, and leaving out nearly everything; Dr. von Heber going through life with a chisel, intent on carving; everybody envying him; the von Heber not seen or realised; his way is carved, he *is* his way﻿ … going ahead further and further away as one listened. His poverty and drudgery behind him, at Winnipeg, amongst the ice. Hoisting himself out of it, *making* himself into a doctor; a graduate of “McGill”﻿ … standing out among the graduates with even the very manner of success more marked in him than in them with their money and ease; sailing to England steady-minded in the awful risk of borrowed money﻿ … it’s wrong, insulting to him to think of it while he is still in the midst of the effort﻿ … a sort of treachery to know the details at all﻿ … the *impossibility* of not dwelling on them. But thinking disperses his general effect. In the strength and sunshine of him there is power. The things he has done are the power in him; no need to know the gossipy details; that was why the facts sounded so familiar; reproachful, as Dr. Hurd brought them out.﻿ …

I knew *all* about him when I met his sunshine. I ought to have rushed away garlanded with hawthorn, with some woman, and waited till he came again. Dr. Hurd looks like an old woman; an old gossip. Old men are worse gossips than old women. They can’t keep their hands off. They make phrases. Dr. Hurd is a dead, dead old woman. Handling things like an old man. It was so natural to listen. “Natural” things get you lost and astray﻿ … kiss-in-the-ring “just a little harmless nonsense﻿ … there’s no harm in a little gay nonsense chickie.” There’s no such thing as harmless nonsense: Dissipation makes you forget everything. Secret sacred places. George and John faithful and steady can’t make those. They smile *personally* and the room or the landscape is immediately silly and tame.﻿ …

===Chinese===
那是在他提议音乐会之后的那一刻开始的。到时候以前我要去买顶新大礼帽。那种对打趣的可怕索求。他那种等待的姿态，仿佛人是什么古怪的造物，他就等着看人说点什么、做点什么人人能懂的东西——同英国人的做派一模一样，只是更敞亮。可那样的英国人不爱音乐，也不让人读书给他们听。也许他的父母属于另一类英国人，他把那一类的印记带在身上，许诺着认真和爱美之心，却被生活逼进那种插科打诨的世故路数里——那种人身上仿佛全然没有神圣的角落。轻快的话语，浸在笑里，堆叠成一句追问：到底怎么*回事*。男人们，索求着打趣和逗乐；女人们，只有靠对一切冷嘲热讽才应付得下去。

冯·黑贝尔是个会*凿*出自己路的人。……天哪。他*了不起*。凿出自己的路；那种让你又满足又不安的短语；短短的，几乎什么都没说全；冯·黑贝尔医生揣着一把凿子走完一生，一心凿下去；人人都羡慕他；那个冯·黑贝尔本体却无人看见、无人领会；他的路凿出来了，他*就是*他的路……听着听着，他越走越远。他身后的贫穷与苦役，在温尼伯（Winnipeg），在冰天雪地里。他从那里把自己拔了出来，*把*自己造成一个医生；“麦吉尔”（McGill）的毕业生……在毕业生堆里鹤立鸡群，连成功的那股气派都在他身上比在那群有钱有闲的人身上更醒目；借着一笔可怕的险钱，心态平稳地渡海去英国……趁他还在奋斗之中去想这些，是错的，是对他的冒犯……知道这些细节本身就是一种背叛……可又*不可能*不去细想。可细想会驱散他总体的效果。他的力量和阳光里有威力。他做成的事就是他身上的威力；用不着知道那些八卦的细节；所以那些事实听着才那么耳熟；带着责备，被赫德医生一桩桩抖出来。……

我遇上他的阳光时，就已经*什么*都知道了。我本该戴着山楂花环跑开，同哪个女人一道，等着他再来。赫德医生像个老太婆；一个搬弄是非的老太婆。老头子比老太婆更爱搬弄是非。他们忍不住要伸手。他们编造漂亮话。赫德医生是个死气沉沉、死气沉沉的老太婆。用老头子的方式摆弄种种事情。听他讲是那么自然。“自然”的东西让你迷失、让你走偏……抢圈亲吻游戏（kiss-in-the-ring）“不过是一点点无伤大雅的胡闹……快活的胡闹一点点，害不了人，小宝贝”。天下没有无伤大雅的胡闹这回事：放荡让你忘掉一切。秘密的神圣角落。忠实稳当的乔治和约翰造不出那些角落。他们*个人化地*一笑，房间或风景立刻就变得又蠢又驯。……

===Original===
“I never met a chap who could make so much of what he knows﻿ … pick up﻿ … and bring them out better than the chap could himself.” The four figures sitting in the little room round the lamp. Dr. Hurd talking his gynæcology simply; a relief, a clear clean place in the world of women’s doctors.﻿ … Dr. Winchester talking for Dr. von Heber, his brown beard and his frock-coat just for the time he was talking before Dr. von Heber had grasped it all, looking like a part of the professional world. Dr. Wayneflete’s white criminal face his little white mouth controlledly mouthing﻿ … Wayneflete’s brilliant; but he’s not got von Heber’s strength nor his manner. He’s quiet though that chap﻿ … he’d do well over here﻿ … that spreads your thoughts about, painfully and wholesomely. Dr. Hurd spreads his thoughts about quite simply.﻿ …

===Chinese===
“我从没见过哪个人能把自己知道的东西发挥成这样……捡起来……讲得比那人自己还讲得好。”四个身影围灯坐在小房间里。赫德医生平平实实讲他的妇科学；一阵宽慰，女人医生的世界里一块清明干净的地方。……温彻斯特医生（Dr. Winchester）替冯·黑贝尔医生讲着，他的棕色胡子和他的长礼服——只在他讲着、冯·黑贝尔医生还没把一切握住的当儿——看起来像职业界的一部分。韦恩弗利特医生（Dr. Wayneflete）那张罪犯似的白脸，他那小小的白嘴克制地嚅动着……韦恩弗利特才华横溢；可他没有冯·黑贝尔的力气，也没有他的气派。不过那家伙安静……他到这边来会很有出息……这把你的思绪摊开来，疼，却有益。赫德医生摊开他的思绪倒全不费力。……

---

===Original===
The moment was so surprising that I forgot it. I always forget the things that surprise me. She was hating me and hating everything. I must have told her I was going away. When I said you can have Bunnikin back she suddenly grew older than I. “Oh *Bunn*ikin.” Their beloved Bunnikin, as smartly dressed as Mrs. Corrie, in the smart country house way and knowing how to gush and behave.﻿ … “Bunnikin’s too *simple*.” Sybil in her blue cotton overall in the amber light in the Louis Quinze drawing-room, one with me, wanting me because I was not simple.﻿ … I thought she hated me all the time because I was not worldly. I should not have known I was not simple unless she had told me; that child.

===Chinese===
那一刻太让人吃惊，我竟把它忘了。我总是忘记让我吃惊的事。她那时恨着我，恨着一切。我一定是告诉她我要走了。当我说邦尼金（Bunnikin）还给你吧，她忽然变得比我还大了。“哦，*邦尼*金。”他们心爱的邦尼金，穿得同科里太太（Mrs. Corrie）一样时新，一身时髦乡间宅第的做派，懂得怎样热情寒暄、怎样举止。……“邦尼金太*天真*了。”西比尔（Sybil）穿着她的蓝布罩衫，立在路易十五式（Louis Quinze）客厅的琥珀色光线里，同我一体，因为我天真不起来才要我。……我一直以为她恨我，因为我不够世故。若不是她告诉我，我原不会知道自己不天真；那个孩子。
