===Original===
They had seen the English country with her. It was more alive to them. They would remember. Dr. von Heber was taking it in, with his best ruminating smile, as a personal possession; seeing it with English eyes. Her last year’s ride through the counties was shared now. It would go to Canada.

“It’s coming all the way from *Bakewell*.”

“Where will that place be?”

“Oh I don’t know; somewhere; in the north I think. Yorkshire. No, the Peak. The Peak district. Peak Freane. They bake splendidly. The further north you get the better they bake.” The scene was swaying forward into newness. Dr. Winchester suddenly began talking about the historical interest of the neighbourhood. They had all been down to look at the Old Curiosity Shop﻿ … there was something about it﻿ … and there was a better local story of their kind. She told Mr. Leyton’s story of the passage in Little Gower Place, body snatchers carrying newly buried bodies through it by night from St. Pancras churchyard to the hospital.

“You don’t say so. To think we’ve gone along there this while and not known.”

“That shop in Lincoln’s Inn isn’t the shop Dickens meant. It’s been pulled down. It’s only the site. Some people think Dickens is sentimental.”

“Those who think so are hypercritical. Besides being sentimental don’t prevent him being one of your very greatest men.”

===Chinese===
他们同她一起看过了英格兰的乡野。对他们，它更活了。他们会记得的。冯·黑贝尔医生正带着他最好的、反刍般的微笑把它收进去，当作一件私人的财物；用英国的眼睛看着它。她去年骑行穿过诸郡的那一路，如今被分享了。它会去到加拿大。

“它正从贝克韦尔（Bakewell）一路赶来呢。”

“那是个什么地方？”

“哦，不知道；什么地方吧；我想在北边。约克郡（Yorkshire）。不，是峰区（the Peak）。峰区。皮克·弗里恩（Peak Freane，饼干牌子，与“峰区”谐音双关）。他们烤东西顶呱呱。越往北走，他们烤得越好。”这一幕正摇荡着进入崭新。温彻斯特医生忽然讲起这一带的历史趣味。他们都下去看过老古玩店（the Old Curiosity Shop）……那地方有点什么……而他们这类故事里还有一个更好的本地版本。她讲了莱顿先生的小高尔广场（Little Gower Place）夹道的故事：盗尸人夜里把新下葬的尸体经它从圣潘克拉斯教堂墓地抬到医院去。

“不会吧。想想我们走了这半天，竟一直不知道。”

“林肯律师学院（Lincoln's Inn）那家店不是狄更斯写的那家。已经拆了。只剩个旧址。有些人认为狄更斯感伤。”

“这么想的人是苛评。再说感伤也不妨碍他是你们最伟大的人物之一。”

===Original===
“*You* should appreciate him highly. If ever there was any man revealed abuses.﻿ … You ought to read our Holmes’ Elsie Venner﻿—We call it his medicated novel over at home” smiled Dr. von Heber. He was speaking low, making a separate conversation. The others were talking together.

“Yes,” murmured Miriam. “I must.” They both smiled a wide agreement. “I’ve got it over at home” murmured Dr. von Heber his smile deepening forwards. You shall read it when you come. *We’ll* read it, he said smiling to himself. She tried to stay where he was, not to be distracted by her thoughts. It must be Holmes’ worst book. A book written on purpose, to prove something.

“Didactic” she said with helpless suddenness, “but I like Holmes’ breakfast books.”

“You’ve read those?”

“Yes” said Miriam wearily. He had caught something from her thoughts. She saw him looking smaller, confined to the passing English present, a passing moment in his determined Canadian life. His strong unconsidered opinions held him through it and would receive and engulf him forever when he went back. Perhaps he had not noticed her thoughts. Well I must bid you a welcome adoo she said getting up to go.

“Now *where*” he smiled rising, and surrounding her with his smile, “where did you discover Artemus *Ward*?”

===Chinese===
“*你们*才该高度重视他。要是有谁揭露过弊政，那就是他。……你该读读我们霍姆斯的《埃尔茜·文纳》（Elsie Venner）——我们在国内管它叫他的药味小说。”冯·黑贝尔医生微笑道。他压低声音说着，另成一场单独的谈话。其余的人正自交谈。

“是的，”米丽亚姆喃喃道。“我一定要读。”两人都展开了宽宽的笑，达成一致。“我家那边有这本书。”冯·黑贝尔医生低声道，笑意向前加深。你来的话就借你读。*我们*一起读，他对自己笑着补了一句。她努力停驻在他所在的地方，不被自己的念头带偏。那准是霍姆斯最差的书。一本存心写的书，为了证明什么。

“说教。”她猝不及防地脱口道，“不过我喜欢霍姆斯的早餐书。”

“那些你也读过？”

“读过。”米丽亚姆倦倦地说。他从她的念头里捕到了什么。她看见他显得小了，被囿于流逝的英国眼下，他那决绝的加拿大人生里一个流逝的瞬间。他那些强硬的、不假思索的意见撑着他渡过此刻，等他回去，就会把他接住、把他永远吞没。也许他没有察觉她的念头。好了，我得向你道一声欢迎的再会了。她起身要走时说。

“那么*究竟*在哪儿，”他起身微笑着，用他的微笑把她围起来，“你是在哪儿发现阿蒂默斯·沃德（Artemus Ward）的？”

## IX / IX

===Original===
It was Mrs. Bailey coming up the top flight clearing her throat. Tapping at the door.

“Ah. I thought the young lady was in. I *thought* so.” Mrs. Bailey stood approving inside the door. The sunlight streamed on to her shabby skirt. The large dusty house, the many downstair rooms, the mysterious dark-roomed vault of the basement, all upright in her upright form; hurried smeary cleansings, swift straightening of grey-sheeted beds, the strange unfailing water-system, gurgling cisterns, gushing taps and lavatory flushes, the wonder of gaslight and bedroom candles, the daily meals magically appearing and disappearing; her knowledge of the various mysteriously arriving and vanishing people, all beginning and ending in her triumphant, reassuring smile that went forward outside beyond these things, with everybody.

Now that she was there, bearing and banishing all these heavy things, the squat green teapot on the table in the blaze of window-light, the Chinese lantern hanging from the hook in the ceiling, the little Madras muslin curtains at either end of the endmost lattices made a picture and set the room free from the challenge of the house accumulating as Miriam had come up through it and preventing the effect she had sought when she put out the green teapot on the sunlit table. She was receiving Mrs. Bailey as a guest, backed up by the summery little window-room. She stood back in the gloom, dropping back into the green lamplit stillness of the farmhouse garden. The Song of Hiawatha sounded on and on amongst the trees, the trunk of the huge sheltering oak lit brightly by the shaded lamp on the little garden table, the forms in the long chairs scarcely visible. She offered Mrs. Bailey the joy of her journey down, her bicycle in the van, Miss Szigmondy’s London guests, the sixteenth-century ingle, the pine-scented bedrooms with sloping floors, the sandy high-banked lanes and pine-clad hills, the strange talk with the connoisseur, the kind stupid boyish mind of the London doctor who had *seen* myopic astigmatism across the lunch table and admitted being beaten in argument without resentment; the long dewy morning ride to Guildford; the happy thorns in her hands keeping the weekend still going on at Wimpole Street; her renewed sense of the simplicity of imposing looking people, their personal helplessness on the surface of wealthy social life; the glow of wealthy social life lighting the little wooden window-room, gleaming from the sheeny flecks of light on the well-shaped green teapot.

===Chinese===
是贝利太太上顶层的楼梯来了，清着嗓子。敲了敲门。

“啊。我就知道小姐在。我*就知道*。”贝利太太站在门内，一副嘉许的神气。阳光泼在她寒酸的裙子上。这幢多尘的大房子，楼下一个又一个房间，地下室那神秘的暗室穹窿，全都直立在她笔直的身形里；匆匆的、抹得花污的清扫，麻利地拉直灰床单的床，那套奇妙的水系永不出错，咕咕作响的水箱，哗哗的水龙头和抽水马桶，煤气灯与卧室蜡烛的奇观，一日三餐魔法般地出现又消失；她对那些神秘来去的人人的知根知底——一切都始于、又终于她那得意的、令人安心的微笑，那微笑越过这一切向前去，同每个人在一起。

好啊。如今她在这里，扛着、也镇着这一切沉重的东西，桌上那只蹲圆的绿茶壶沐在窗光的烈焰里，天花板挂钩上垂着中国灯笼，最末一格格子窗两头挂着小小的马德拉斯细纱帘，合成了一幅画，把房间从那挑战里解放出来——那挑战是房子一路攒下的，米丽亚姆上楼穿过它时一路聚积，眼看要毁掉她把绿茶壶摆上洒满阳光的桌子时想要的那份效果。此刻她是在接待一位来做客的贝利太太，背后有这间夏天似的小小窗屋撑腰。她退进暗处，退回那农家花园绿色的、灯下的静止里。《海华沙之歌》（The Song of Hiawatha）在树间一程程响着，那棵巨大的庇荫橡树的树干，被小花园桌上罩了灯罩的灯照得通明，长椅上的人影几乎看不见。她把她的下乡之乐呈给贝利太太：她的自行车搁在行李车里，齐格蒙迪小姐的伦敦客人们，十六世纪的壁炉（ingle），松香味的、地板倾斜的卧室，沙土的、高埂的车道和披满松树的山丘，同那位鉴赏家的奇怪谈话，那位和善愚蠢、孩子气的伦敦医生隔着午餐桌*看*出了近视性散光、又在辩论输了之后毫无怨色；清晨踏着露水的长途骑行去吉尔福德（Guildford）；掌心里快活的小刺，让那个周末还在温波尔街继续着；她重新感到，那些仪表堂堂的人其实简单，在阔绰社交生活的表面上一个个束手无策；阔绰社交生活的光晕照亮这间小小的木窗屋，从那只匀称的绿茶壶上莹莹的光斑里闪出来。

===Original===
Mrs. Bailey advanced to the middle of the floor and stood looking towards the window. My word aren’t we *smart* she breathed.

“I like the teapot and the lantern, don’t you?” said Miriam.

“Very pretty, mts, very pretty, young lady.”

“It reminds me of weekends. It *is* a weekend. That is my drawing-room.”

“That’s it. It’s a weekend,” beamed Mrs. Bailey. But she had come for something. The effect was not spoiled by giving a wrong, *social* impression of it, because Mrs. Bailey was busily thinking behind her voice. When she had gone the silent effect would be there, more strongly. Perhaps she had some new suggestion to make about Sissie.

===Chinese===
贝利太太走到地板中央，站着朝窗子望去。我的天，咱们可真*神气*。她轻声说。

“我喜欢这茶壶和灯笼，您呢？”米丽亚姆说。

“真好看，啧啧，真好看，小姐。”

“它让我想起一个个周末。这*就是*一个周末。这就是我的客厅。”

“正是。这就是个周末。”贝利太太眉开眼笑。可她是为一件什么事来的。给这件事一个错误的、*社交*性的印象，并不损这份效果，因为贝利太太正在她的嗓音后面忙着想事情。等她走了，那无声的效果还会在，而且更强。说不定她对西西又有什么新的提议。

===Original===
“Well, young lady, I want to talk to you.” Mrs. Bailey propped one elbow on the mantelpiece and brushed at her skirt. Miriam waited, watching her impatiently. The Tansley Street life was fading into the glow of the oncoming holiday season. Rain was cooling the July weather, skirmishy sunlit April rain and wind drawing her forward. There was leisure in cool uncrowded streets and restaurants and in the two cool houses, no pressure of work, the gay easy August that was almost as good as a holiday, and the certainty beyond the rain, of September brilliance.

“Well you know I’ve a great regard for you, young lady.”

Miriam stared back at the long row of interviews with Mrs. Bailey and sought her face for her invisible thoughts.

“Well to come straight to the point without beating about the bush, it’s about him, that little man, you know who I mean.”

===Chinese===
“是这样，小姐，我想跟您谈谈。”贝利太太把一只手肘支在壁炉架上，掸了掸裙子。米丽亚姆等着，不耐烦地看着她。坦斯利街的生活正褪进将临的假期季节的光晕里。雨水在给七月的天降着温，四月式的、散散点点带着阳光的雨和风牵着她向前。清凉而不拥挤的街道和餐馆里、那两幢清凉的房子里都有闲暇，没有工作的压力，那个轻快安逸的八月几乎抵得上一个假期，还有雨那头必定的、九月的明丽。

“这个您是知道的，我对您可是真心敬重，小姐。”

米丽亚姆回望着同贝利太太的一长串谈话，在她脸上寻找她那看不见的心思。

“那我就开门见山，不绕弯子了，是为他，那个小个子男人，您知道我说的是谁。”

===Original===
“Who?”

“Mendizzable.”

Miriam’s interest awoke and flared. That past patch of happy life had been somehow or other visible to Mrs. Bailey. She felt decorated and smiled into the room.

“Well; you know I don’t believe in talk going about from one to another. In *my* opinion people should mind their own business and not listen to tittle-tattle, or if they do, keep it to themselves without passing it on and making mischief.”

“Has someone been trying to make mischief about poor little Mr. Mendizabal?”

“Well, if it was about him I wouldn’t mind so much. Little villain. That’s my name for him.”

===Chinese===
“谁？”

“门迪扎布尔。”

米丽亚姆的兴趣醒了，腾地亮起来。过去那一小片快乐的生活，竟不知怎么让贝利太太看见了。她觉得自己像受了勋，朝房间里微笑。

“是这样；您知道，我不赞成话在人与人之间传来传去。照*我*的意见，人们该管好自己的事，别去听那些闲言碎语，就算听了，也该闷在自己肚里，别再往下传、惹是非。”

“是不是有人在拿可怜的小个子门迪萨尔先生搬弄是非？”

“唉，要真是关于他的，我倒不那么在意。小坏蛋。我私下就这么叫他。”

===Original===
“Fascinating little villain if he must be called a villain.”

“Well; that’s what I’ve got to ask you my chahld; are you under a fascination about him? You’ll excuse me asking such a question.”

Solicitude! *what* for?

“Well. I *did* think him fascinating; he fascinated me, he would anybody. He would fascinate Miss *Scott* if he chose.”

“’Er? ’Er be fascinated by anybody? She thinks too much of number one for that.”

… Miss Scott. Dressing so carefully, so full of independent talk and laughter and not able to be fascinated﻿ … too farseeing to be fascinated.

===Chinese===
“要是一定得叫他坏蛋，那也是个迷人的小坏蛋。”

“是这样；我要问您的就是这个，我的孩子；您是不是叫他迷住了？您要原谅我问这么一个问题。”

关切！为*什么*？

“这个嘛。我*确实*觉得他迷人；他迷住过我，迷得住任何人。他要是愿意，连斯科特*小姐*都迷得住。”

“她？她会叫人迷住？她把自己看得太重，迷不住的。”

……斯科特小姐。穿得那么经心，满嘴独立的谈吐和笑，是叫人迷不住的……太有远见，迷不住。

===Original===
“But why do you ask? I’m not responsible for Mr. Mendizabal’s being a fascinating little man.”

“Fascinating little *devil*. You should have heard Dr. Winchester.”

Something hidden; all the time; behind the politeness of the house.

“Dr. *Win*chester?”

“Dr. Winchester. Do you remember him coming out into the hall one evening when you were brushing your coat?”

“And brushing it for me. Yes.”

===Chinese===
“可您为什么问这个？门迪萨尔先生是个迷人的小个子，这可不由我负责。”

“迷人的小*魔鬼*。您真该听听温彻斯特医生怎么说。”

有什么东西藏着；一直藏着；在这幢房子的客气后面。

“温*切*斯特医生？”

“温彻斯特医生。您记不记得有天晚上您在门厅刷外套，他走了出来？”

“还替我刷。记得。”

===Original===
“He didn’t know how to let you go.” There was a trembling in Mrs. Bailey’s voice. “He said,” she pursued breathlessly, “he was in two minds to come with you himself.”

“*Where? Why?*”

“Why? He *knew* that fella was waiting for you round the corner.”

Suddenly appearing, brushing so carefully﻿ … why not have spoken and come.

“Well now we’re coming to it. I can’t tell you how it all happened, that’s between Mr. Gunner and Miss S. They got to know you was going out with Mendizzable and where you went. It’s contemptible I know, if you like, but there’s many such people about.”

Miriam checked her astonishment, making a mental note for future contemplation of the spectacle of Mr. Gunner, or Miss Scott, following her to Ruscino’s. They had told Mrs. Bailey and talked to the doctors.﻿ … Spies; talking idle; maliciously picking over her secret life.

===Chinese===
“他不知道怎么放您走。”贝利太太的嗓音里有一丝颤抖。“他说，”她屏着气接着说，“他一时拿不定主意，要不要亲自陪您去。”

“*去哪儿？为什么？*”

“为什么？他*知道*那个家伙在拐角等您。”

突然出现，刷得那么经心……为什么不开口、不一起来呢。

“这就说到正题了。事情怎么都传出来的，我说不上，那是冈纳先生和斯小姐之间的事。他们打听到您同门迪扎布尔出去，还打听到你们去了哪儿。我知道这下作，您要这么说的话，可这种人有的是。”

米丽亚姆按住她的惊愕，在心里记下一笔，留待日后细想那幅图景：冈纳先生，或者斯科特小姐，一路跟踪她到鲁西诺。他们告诉了贝利太太，还同医生们谈过。……密探；闲磕牙；恶意地翻检她的秘密生活。

===Original===
“Dr. Winchester said he was worried half out of his senses about you.”

“Why not have said so?”

“You may be wondering,” Mrs. Bailey flushed a girlish pink, “why I come up today telling you all this. That’s just what *I* say. That’s just the worst of it. He never breathed a word to me till he went.”

Dr. Winchester *gone*﻿ … the others gone﻿ … of *course*. Next week would be August. They had all vanished away; out of the house, back to Canada. Dr. von Heber gone without a word. Perhaps *he* had been worried. They *all* had. That was why they had all been so nice and surrounding.﻿ … That was the explanation of everything.﻿ … They were brothers. Jealous brothers. The first she had had. This was the sort of thing girls had who had brothers. Cheek. If only she had known and shown them how silly they were.

“Lawk. I wish to goodness he’d come straight to me at once.”

===Chinese===
“温彻斯特医生说，他为您担心得魂儿都丢了一半。”

“那为什么不说？”

“您也许在纳闷，”贝利太太涨起少女似的粉红，“我今天怎么上来讲这些。我说的正是这个。最糟的正是这个。他直到走，一个字也没跟我吐过。”

温彻斯特医生*走了*……其余的也走了……*当然*。下星期就是八月了。他们全都不见了；出了这幢房子，回加拿大去了。冯·黑贝尔医生一声不响地走了。也许*他*也在担心。他们*全*都在担心。所以他们才都那么好、那么众星拱月。……这就是对一切的解释。……他们是兄长。嫉妒的兄长。她头一回有了兄长。有兄长的姑娘们过的就是这种日子。放肆。她要是早知道，让他们看看自己有多蠢就好了。

“老天。我真巴不得他干脆直接来找我了事。”

===Original===
“Well. It’s awfully sweet of them from their point of view. They were such *awfully* nice little men in their way.”﻿ … *Why* didn’t they come to me, instead of all this talk? They knew me well enough. All those long talks at night. And all the time they were seeing a foolish girl fascinated by a disreputable foreigner. How dare they?

“That’s what I say. I can’t forgive him for that. They’re all alike. Selfish.”

“All old men like Dr. Winchester are selfish. Selfish and weak. They get to think of nothing but their comforts. And keep out of everything by talk.”

“It’s not him I mean. It’s the other one.”

“Which?” What was Mrs. Bailey going to say? What? Miriam gazed angrily.

===Chinese===
“唉。从他们的角度看，他们好心得不得了。他们那种人，真是*顶顶*好的小个子男人。”……*为什么*他们不来找我，偏要这样背后议论？他们跟我够熟了。那么多回深夜长谈。而他们自始至终看见的，是一个傻姑娘，被一个名声可疑的外国人迷住了。他们怎么敢？

“我说的就是这个。这一点我不能原谅他。他们全是一个样。自私。”

“温彻斯特医生那样的老头子全都自私。又自私又软弱。他们到后来心里只装着自己的安逸。靠说话躲开一切。”

“我说的不是他。是另一位。”

“哪一位？”贝利太太要说什么？什么？米丽亚姆怒视着。

===Original===
“That’s what I must tell you. That’s why I asked you if you was under a fascination.”

“Oh well, they’ve gone. What does it matter?”

“I feel I ought to tell you. He, von Heber, had made up his mind to *speak*. He was one in a thousand, Winchester said. She’s lost von Heber he said. He thought the *world* of her, ’e sez,” gasped Mrs. Bailey. “My *word*, I wish I’d known what was going on.”

Miriam flinched. Mrs. Bailey must be made to go now.

“Oh really,” she said in trembling tones. “He was an awfully nice man.”

“My word. Isn’t it a pity,” said Mrs. Bailey with tears in her eyes. “It worries me something shocking.”

“Oh well, if he was so stupid.”

===Chinese===
“这正是我必须告诉您的。所以我才问您是不是叫人迷住了。”

“唉，算了，他们都走了。还有什么要紧？”

“我觉得我该告诉您。他，冯·黑贝尔，已经打定主意要*开口*了。千里挑一的人物，温彻斯特说的。她把冯·黑贝尔错过了，他说。他把她说上了*天*，他说，”贝利太太喘着气说。“我的*天*，我真希望我早知道这些。”

米丽亚姆瑟缩了一下。现在必须让贝利太太走了。

“哦，真的，”她用发颤的声音说。“他是个非常好的男人。”

“我的天。多可惜啊，”贝利太太眼里含着泪说。“这事愁得我不行。”

“唉，谁让他那么蠢。”

===Original===
“Well, you can’t blame him after what Mendizzable *said*.”

“You haven’t told me.”

“He said he’d only to raise his finger. Oh Lawk. Well there you are, now you’ve got it all.”

Mrs. Bailey *must* go. Mr. Mendizabal’s mind was a French novel. He’d said French thoughts in English to the doctors. They had believed. Even Canadian men can have French minds.

“Yes. Well I see it all now. Mr. Mendizabal’s vanity is his own affair.﻿ … I’m sure I hope they’ve all had an interesting summer. I’m awfully glad you’ve told me. It’s most interesting.”

“Well, I felt it was my duty to come up and tell you. I felt you ought to know.”

“Yes﻿ … I’m awfully glad you’ve told me. It’s like, er, a storm in a teacup.”

===Chinese===
“唉，门迪扎布尔*说了那些话*之后，您也不能怪他。”

“您还没告诉我呢。”

“他说，他只要抬一抬手指头。唉老天。好了，就是这些，如今您全知道了。”

贝利太太*必须*走了。门迪萨尔先生的头脑是一部法国小说。他用英语对医生们说了法语的思想。他们信了。连加拿大男人也可以长着法国的头脑。

“是的。这下我全明白了。门迪萨尔先生的虚荣是他自己的事。……我相信，他们都过了一个有意思的夏天。您告诉我这些，我太高兴了。有意思极了。”

“唉，我觉得我有责任上来告诉您。我觉得您应该知道。”

“是啊……您告诉我，我太高兴了。这就像，呃，茶杯里的风暴。”

===Original===
“It’s not them I’m thinking of. Lot of low-minded gossips. That’s my opinion. It’s the harm they do I’m thinking of.”

“They can’t do any harm. As for the doctors they’re quite able to take care of themselves.” Miriam moved impatiently about the room. But she could not let herself look at her thoughts with Mrs. Bailey there.

“Well young lady,” murmured Mrs. Bailey dolorously at last, “I felt I couldn’t do less than come up, for my *own* satisfaction.”

She thinks I have made a scandal, without consulting her﻿ … her mind flew, flaming, over the gossiping household, over Mrs. Bailey’s thoughts as she pondered the evidence. Wrenching away from the spectacle she entrenched herself far off; clutching out towards the oblivion of the coming holidays; a clamour came up from the street, the swaying tumult of a fire-engine, the thunder of galloping horses, the hoarse shouts of the firemen; the outside life to which she went indifferent to any grouped faces either of approval or of condemnation.

===Chinese===
“我想的不是他们。一帮心思下作的碎嘴子。这是我的看法。我想的是他们造的害。”

“他们造不了什么害。至于医生们，他们完全照管得了自己。”米丽亚姆不耐烦地在屋里走动。可贝利太太在，她没法让自己去看自己的念头。

“唉，小姐，”贝利太太末了凄凄地喃喃道，“我觉得我怎么也该上来一趟，为*我自己的*安心。”

她以为我闹了一桩丑闻，还不曾请教过她……她的心腾着火，飞过这个搬弄是非的人家，飞过贝利太太掂量证据时的心思。她从这幅景象里猛地挣开，把自己远远地安进壕垒；朝将临的假期的忘却伸出手去；街上响起一阵喧嚷，救火车摇摇晃晃的轰鸣，奔马的雷声，消防队员嘶哑的呼喊；那种外面的生活，她朝它走去，对任何聚拢来的面孔——赞许的也好、谴责的也好——一概无动于衷。

===Original===
“I’m awfully sorry you’ve had all this, Mrs. Bailey.”

“Oh that’s nothing. It’s not that I think of.”

“Don’t think about anything. It doesn’t matter.”

“Well I’ve got it off my mind now I’ve spoken.”

“It *is* abominable isn’t it. Never mind. I don’t care. People are perfectly welcome to talk about me if it gives them any satisfaction.”

“That is so. It’s von Heber I’m so mad about.”

“They’re all alike as you say.”

“He might have given you a chance.”

===Chinese===
“这些事都落在您身上，我实在过意不去，贝利太太。”

“哦，那不算什么。我想的不是这个。”

“什么都别想了。没有关系。”

“唉，说出来了，我心里也卸下了。”

“这*确实*可恶，是不是。别介意。我不在乎。人们尽管来议论我好了，只要能给他们一点满足。”

“说的是。我气的是冯·黑贝尔。”

“就像您说的，他们全一个样。”

“他本该给您一个机会的。”

===Original===
Dr. von *Heber*; suddenly nearer than anyone. Her own man. By his own conviction. Found away here, at Mrs. Bailey’s; Mrs. Bailey’s regret measuring his absolute genuineness. Gone away.﻿ …

She steadied herself to say “Oh, if he’s selfish.”

“They’re all that, every one of them. But we’ve all got to settle in life, sooner or later.”

That was all, for Mrs. Bailey. She rallied woefully in the thought that Mrs. Bailey knew she could have settled in life if she had chosen.

Flickering faintly far away was something to be found behind all this, some silent thing she would find by herself if only Mrs. Bailey would go.

===Chinese===
冯·*黑贝尔*医生；忽然比谁都近。她自己的人。凭他自己的信念。在这遥远的地方，在贝利太太这里找到的；贝利太太的惋惜量出了他绝对的真。走远了。……

她稳住自己，说：“哦，要是他自私的话。”

“他们全那样，个个都是。可我们谁都得在生活里安顿下来，早晚的事。”

对贝利太太来说，就这些了。她凄惶地振作起来，想着：贝利太太知道，她只要愿意，本也是能在生活里安顿下来的。

远远的什么地方，有什么在幽幽地闪，要在这全部的后面才找得到——某样无声的东西，只要贝利太太肯走，她会自己找到。

---

===Original===
*Fascinated.* How did they find the word? It was true; and false. This was the way people talked. These were the true-false phrases used to sum up things for which there were no words.

They had no time. They were too busy. That was in the scheme. They were somehow prevented from doing anything. Dr. von Heber had been saved. The fascinating eyes and snorting smile had saved him; coming out of space to tell him she was a flirt. He had boasted. She adore me; hah! I tell you she adore me, he would say. It was history repeating itself. Max and Ted. Again after all these years. A *Jew*.

===Chinese===
*迷住了。*他们是怎么找到这个词的？它是真的；也是假的。人们就是这么说话的。这些真真假假的短语，专用来总括那些没有词可说的事物。

他们没有时间。他们太忙。这就在那套安排里。他们不知怎么被拦着，什么都做不成。冯·黑贝尔医生得救了。那双迷人的眼睛和喷鼻的微笑救了他；从虚空里出来告诉他，她是个卖弄风情的女人。他吹嘘过。她崇拜我；哈！我告诉你她崇拜我，他会这么说。历史在重演。马克斯（Max）和特德（Ted）。隔了这么多年，又来一回。一个*犹太人*。

---

===Original===
The unconscious, inexorable ship﻿ … gliding across the Atlantic. They would take up their bright Canadian life again. England, a silent picture, fading.﻿ … Dear Dr. von Heber. I owe it to myself just to inform you that the legend you heard about me was untrue. Wishing you a happy and prosperous career yours truly. That would be saying I, fool, have discovered too late that I was not clever enough to let you imagine that you were the only kind of man in the world﻿ … discreet women are sly. To get on in the world it is necessary to be sly. Von Heber is sly. Careful and prudent and sly. What did genius Wayneflete think? Genius understands everything. Discreet proper clever women are open books to him. He will never marry. Whimsical old failure, Winchester, disappearing into British Columbia; failure; decorated in his evening conversations by having been to England.﻿ … My dear von Heber, what the devil do you mean? When will you meet me? Choose your own weapons﻿ … that would be admitting not having the right to be as free and indiscreet as one chooses﻿ … “a woman must march with her regiment; if she is wise she does”; something like that. If a woman is *sly* she marches with her regiment﻿ … all in agreement, being sly and discreet, helping each other. What for? What was the plot for?﻿ … there’s a *word*﻿ … *coercion*, that’s the word. Better any sort of free life.

===Chinese===
那艘无意识的、不容分说的船……滑过大西洋。他们将重新拾起他们明亮的加拿大生活。英格兰，一幅无声的画，褪色中。……亲爱的冯·黑贝尔医生。为我自己计，谨此奉告：您听到的关于我的传说不实。祝您事业顺遂、繁荣昌盛，您忠实的。这等于说：我，傻子，发现得太迟，我不够聪明，没能让您继续想象您是世界上唯一的那种男人……端庄的女人是狡猾的。要在世上出人头地，就必须狡猾。冯·黑贝尔就狡猾。细心、审慎、狡猾。天才韦恩弗利特是怎么想的？天才什么都懂。端庄规矩的聪明女人在他眼里是摊开的书。他永远不会结婚。古怪的老失败者温彻斯特，正消失进不列颠哥伦比亚；失败者；凭着一趟英国之行，给他的晚间谈话添了勋章。……我亲爱的冯·黑贝尔，你到底是什么意思？你什么时候见我？武器随你挑……那就等于承认：人没有权利想多自由、想多不检点就多自由多不检点……“女人必须跟着她的团队行军；她要是聪明，就会这么做”；大意如此。女人要是*狡猾*，她就跟着团队行军……全体一致，又狡猾又端庄，互相帮衬。为了什么？这场合谋是为了什么？……有这么一个*词*……*强制*（coercion），就是这个词。随便哪种自由的生活都更好。

---

===Original===
If he could have *seen*. But then he would have seen those other moments too. Von Heber. Power and success. Never any moments like that. Divided life all the time *always*. So much for his profession, so much for her, outside it with the regiment of women. Proper men can’t bring the wild, gleaming﻿ … channel of flowers, pulling dragging to fling yourself headlong down it and awake, dead. Dead if you do. Dead if you don’t. Now Tomlinson gave up the ghost.

===Chinese===
他要是能*看见*就好了。可那样他也会看见另外那些时刻。冯·黑贝尔。权力与成功。从来不会有那样的时刻。永远永远过着分成的日子。一份给他的职业，一份给她，在职业之外，同女人的团队在一起。规矩的男人端不来那条野性的、灼灼闪光的……花的河道，拽着拖着，把你自己一头掷下去，然后醒来，死了。做也是死。不做也是死。汤姆林森就此咽了气（Now Tomlinson gave up the ghost，吉卜林诗行）。

---

===Original===
“You’re just in time.” They had come back? He had come back for something?

“There’s a surprise waiting for you upstairs”; *what* surprise Mrs. Bailey; how can you be happy and mysterious; cajoling to rush on into nothing, sweeping on, talking; “a friend of yorse; Dr. Winchester’s room; she’s longing to see you.”

“Good heavens.”

Miriam fled upstairs and tapped at the door of the room below her own. A smooth fluting thoughtful voice answered tranquilly from within the spaces of the room behind the closed door. There was no one with a voice like that to speak to intimately. It was a stranger, someone she had met somewhere and given the address to; a superior worldly person serenely answering the knock of a housemaid. She went in. Tall figure, tall skirt and blouse standing at the dressing-table. The grime-screened saffron light fell on white hands pinning a skein of bright gold hair round the back of a small head. How do you do, Miriam announced, coming forward with obedient reluctance. The figure turned; a bent flushed face laughed from tumbled hair.

“’Ere I am dear; turned up like a bad penny. I’ll shake ’ands in a minute.” With compressed lips and bent frowning brow Miss Dear went on busily pinning. “Bother my silly hair,” she went on with deepening flush, “I shall be able to talk to you in a minute.”

===Chinese===
“您来得正是时候。”他们回来了？还是他回来取什么东西？

“楼上有个惊喜等着您呢”；*什么*惊喜，贝利太太；您怎么能又高兴又神秘；哄着人往前冲进虚无里，一路滔滔；“您的一位朋友；温彻斯特医生的房间；她正盼着见您呢。”

“老天爷。”

米丽亚姆逃上楼，敲了敲她楼下那间房的门。一个平滑如笛、若有所思的嗓音，从关着的门内、从房间的纵深里安然应了一声。再没有谁的嗓音是这样的，可以拿来亲密地说话。是个陌生人，她某处见过、给过地址的什么人；一位上流的、世故的人物，安详地应着女仆的叩门。她走进去。高高的身影，高高的裙子和罩衫立在梳妆台前。隔着尘网滤成的藏红花色光线，落在两只白手上，正把一绺亮金色的头发往一颗小小的头颅后面别。您好。米丽亚姆通报着，顺从而不情愿地走上前。那身影转过身；一张俯着的、涨红的脸从蓬乱的头发里笑出来。

“我到啦，亲爱的；像枚甩不脱的晦气铜板，又转回来了。我马上就跟你握手。”迪尔小姐抿着唇、蹙着眉，手上忙着别发针。“讨厌的破头发，”她越来越红地接着说，“我马上就能跟你说话了。”

===Original===
Miriam clutched at the amazed resentment that flamed from her up and down the sudden calm unconscious façade reared between her and the demolished house, spread across the very room that had held the key to its destruction. She fought for annihilating words, but her voice had spoken ahead of her.

“*El*eanor!”

With the word a soft beauty ran flickering, an edge of light about the form searched by her gazing eyes. Their shared past flowed in the room﻿ … the skirt was a shabby thin blue serge, rubbed shiny, the skimpy cotton blouse had an ugly greyish stripe and badly cut shoulders, one and eleven at an awful shop, but she was just going to speak.

“There that’s better,” she said lowering her hands to tweak at the blouse, her blue eyes set judiciously on the face of the important Duchesse mirror, her passing servant. “’Ow *are* you, dear?”

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆一把攥住那惊愕的怨愤——它顺着那骤然立起的、安然不觉的立面，在她和那座坍塌的房子之间腾起火来，摊满在这间恰恰握着坍塌之钥的房间里。她搏斗着，要找一句毁灭性的话，可她的嗓音已抢在她前头开了口。

“*埃莉*诺！”

这一个词出口，一层柔软的美流光似地掠过，在她凝望的眼睛细细搜寻的那具身影四周，亮起一道光的边。她们共享的过去流进房间里……那条裙子是寒素的薄蓝呢，磨得发亮，那件窄小的棉布罩衫带着一道难看的灰条纹，肩裁得糟糕，一先令十一便士的糟心铺子里的货，可她正要开口说话。

“好了，这样好些。”她说着垂下手去扯了扯罩衫，蓝眼睛端端正正地落在那面气派的公爵夫人式穿衣镜（Duchesse mirror）的镜面上——她过身的仆人。“你*到底*怎么样，亲爱的？”

===Original===
“*I’m* all right;” thrilled Miriam, “you’re just in time for dinner.”

“I am afraid I don’t look very *din*nery,” frowned Miss Dear, fingering the loose unshapely collar of her blouse. “I wonder if you could let me have a tie, just for today, dear.”

“I’ve got a lace one, but it’s crumply,” hazarded Miriam.

“I can manage it I daresay if you’d let me avit.”

===Chinese===
“*我*挺好的；”米丽亚姆兴奋地说，“你来得正好赶上晚饭。”

“只怕我这一身不大*像赴宴的*。”迪尔小姐皱着眉，手指摸着罩衫那圈松垮变形的领子。“不知道你能不能借我一条领带，就今天一天，亲爱的。”

“我有一条蕾丝的，可是皱。”米丽亚姆试着说。

“我想我能对付，只要你肯借我。”

===Original===
The gong sounded. “I shan’t be a second,” Miriam promised and fled. The little stair-flight and her landing, the sunset gilded spaces of her room flung her song out into the world. The tie was worse than she had thought, its middle length crushed and grubby. She hesitated over a card of small pearl-headed lace pins, newly bought and forgotten. For fourpence three farthings the twelve smooth filmy pearl heads, their bright sharp-pointed gilt shanks pinned in a perfect even row through the neat oblong of the sheeny glazed card, lit up her drawer, bringing back the lace-hung aisles of the west-end shop, its counters spread with the fascinating details of the worldly life. The pins were the forefront of her armoury, still too blissfully new to be used.﻿ … However Eleanor arranged the tie she could not use more than three.

“Thank you dear,” she said indifferently, as if they were her own things obligingly brought in, and swiftly pinned one end of the unexamined tie to her blouse collar. With lifted chin she deftly bound the lace round and round close to her neck each swathe firmly pinned, making a column wider than the width of the lace. Above her blouse, transformed by the disappearance of its ugly collar, her graceful neck went up, a column of filmy lace. Miriam watched, learning and amazed.

“That’s better than nothing anyhow,” said Miss Dear from her sideways movements of contemplation. Three or four small pearly heads gleamed mistily from the shapely column of lace. The glazed card lay on the dressing-table crumpled and rent and empty of all its pins.

===Chinese===
锣响了。“我一眨眼就回来。”米丽亚姆许诺着，逃了出去。短短的一段楼梯和她的小平台，她房间里落日镀金的空阔，把她的歌抛进世界。那条领带比她想的还糟，中段压得又皱又脏。她在一板小小的珍珠头蕾丝针前犹豫，新买的，买了就忘了。四便士三法新，十二枚光滑的、薄纱似的珍珠头，它们亮而尖利的镀金针脚，齐齐整整一排别在光洁的卡片那规整的长方形上，照亮了她的抽屉，带回西区店铺里挂满蕾丝的长廊，柜台上摊着世俗生活的种种迷人细节。这些针是她武库的前阵，还幸福得簇新，舍不得用。……可无论埃莉诺怎么摆弄那条领带，她用掉的不会超过三枚。

“谢谢你，亲爱的。”她淡淡地说，仿佛那是她自家的东西，人家好意送了进来，一面飞快地把那条看也没看的领带一端别上罩衫领子。她扬着下巴，麻利地把蕾丝一圈圈贴着脖颈绕上去，每一圈都别牢，绕出一根比蕾丝本身更宽的柱子。罩衫上方，那圈难看的领子一消失，她优雅的颈项便直直而上，成了一根薄纱似的蕾丝的柱。米丽亚姆看着，又受教又惊叹。

“总比没有强。”迪尔小姐侧着头端详着说。三两枚小小的珍珠头，从那根匀称的蕾丝柱上朦朦胧胧地发亮。那张光洁的卡片躺在梳妆台上，皱了，破了，空了，一枚针也不剩。

---

===Original===
The dining-room was a buzz of conversation. The table was packed save for two chairs on Mrs. Bailey’s right hand. *Mrs. Bailey* was wearing a *black satin blouse* cut in a V and a piece of black ribbon-velvet tied round her neck! She was in conversation, preening and arching as she ladled out the soup, with a little *lady* and a big old *gentleman* with a patriarch beard sitting on her right bowing and smiling, personally, towards Miriam and Miss Dear as they took their seats. Miriam bowed and gazed as they went on talking. The old gentleman had a large oblong head above a large expensive spread of smooth well-cut black coat; a huge figure, sitting tall, with easily moving head reared high, massy grey hair; unspectacled smiling glistening eyes and oblong fresh cheeked face wreathed in smiles revealing gleaming squares of gold stopping in his front teeth. His voice was vast and silky, like the beard that moved as he spoke, shifting about on the serviette tucked by one corner into his neck. His little wife was like a kind bird, soft curtains of greying black hair crimping down from a beautifully twisted topknot on either side of a clear gentle forehead. Softly gleaming eyes shone through rimless pince-nez perched delicately on her delicate nose, no ugly straight bar, a little half-hoop to join them together and at the side a delicate gold chain tucked over one ear﻿ … she was about as old as mother had been﻿ … she was exactly like her﻿ … girlishly young, but untroubled; the little white ringed left hand with strange unfamiliarly expressive fingertips and curiously mobile turned-back thumb-tip was herself in miniature. It held a little piece of bread, peaked, expressively, as she ate her soup. She was utterly familiar, no stranger; always known. Miriam adored, seeking her eyes till she looked, and meeting a gentle enveloping welcome, making no break in her continuous soft animation. The only strange thing was a curious *circular* sweep of her delicate jaw as she spoke; a sort of wide mouthing on some of her many quiet words, thrown in through and between and together with the louder easily audible silky tones of her husband. Mrs. Bailey sat unafraid, expanding in happiness. You *will* have a number of things to see she was saying. We are counting on this laddie to be our guide, said the old gentleman turning hugely to his further neighbour. Miriam’s eyes followed and met the face of Dr. *Hurd*﻿ … *grinning*; his intensest brick-red grin. He had not gone! These were his *parents*. *He* needs a holiday too, the dear lad, said the old gentleman laying a hand on his shoulder. Dr. Hurd grinned a rueful disclaimer with his eyes still on Miriam’s and said I shan’t be sorry, his face crinkling with his unexploded hysterically leaping laugh. Mrs. Hurd’s smiling little face flickered with quickly smothered sadness. They had come all the way from Canada to share his triumph and were here smoothing his defeat.﻿ … Canadian old people. A *Canadian* woman﻿ … that circular jaw movement was made by the Canadian vowels. They disturbed a woman’s small mouth more than a man’s. It must affect her thoughts, the held-open mouth; *airing* them; making them *circular*, sympathetically balanced, easier to go on from than the more narrowly mouthed English speech.﻿ … Mr. Gunner, sitting beside your son is a violinist.﻿ … Ah. We shall hope to hear him. Mr. Gunner, small and shyly smiling, next to him an *enormous* woman with a large schoolgirl face, fair straight and schoolgirl hair lifted in a flat wave from her broad forehead into an angry peak, angrily eating with quickly moving brawny arms coming out of elbow sleeves with cheap cream lace frilling, reluctantly forced to flop against the brawny arms. Sallow good-looking husband, olive, furious, cocksure, bilious type, clubby and knowing, flat ignorance on the top of his unconscious shiny round black skull, both snatching at scraps of Scott and Sissie and Gunner chaff, trying to smile their way in to hide their fury with each other. Too poor to get further away from each other, accustomed to boarding house life, eating rapidly and looking for more. She had several brothers; a short aristocratic upper lip and shapely scornful nostrils, brothers in the diplomatic service or the army. There was someone this side of the table they recognised as different and were watching; a tall man beyond Mrs. Barrow, a strange *fine* voice with wandering protesting inflections; speaking out into the world, with practised polished wandering inflections, like a tired pebble worn by the sea, going on and on, presenting the same worn wandering curves wherever it was, always a stranger everywhere, always anew presenting the strange wandering inflections; indiscriminately. That end of the table was not aware of the Hurds. Its group was wandering outside the warm glow of Canadian society. Eleanor Dear was feeling at its doors, pathetic-looking with delicate appealing head and thoughtful baby brow downcast. Us’ll wander out this evening shall us, murmured Miriam in a lover-like undertone. It was a grimace at the wide-open door of Canadian life; an ironic kick à la Harriet. Her heart beat recklessly round the certainty of writing and posting her letter. If he cared he would understand. Mrs. Hurd had come to show her Canadian society, brushing away the tangles and stains of accidental contacts; putting everything right. Of course we will, bridled Miss Dear rebuking her vulgarity. Nothing mattered now but filling up the time.

===Chinese===
餐室里是一片谈话的嗡嗡。桌子坐满了，只余贝利太太右手边两把空椅。*贝利太太*穿着一件*黑缎罩衫*，开成 V 形，颈上还系了一条黑丝绒缎带！她一边舀汤，一边谈笑着梳弄自己、弓起自己，同桌的是一位小小的*夫人*和一位大个子老*绅士*，绅士留着族长般的胡须，坐在她右手，朝落座的米丽亚姆和迪尔小姐个人化地躬身微笑。米丽亚姆鞠了一躬，望着他们继续谈。老绅士有一颗长大的长方头颅，衬着一大片昂贵的、裁剪妥帖的黑呢；身形巨大，坐得高，轻易移动的头高高昂着，一头厚重的灰发；不戴眼镜、笑意盈盈、湿润发亮的眼睛，长方而气色新鲜的脸含笑绽开，露出门牙上一方一方闪光的金质嵌补。他的嗓音浩大而如丝，像他那把说话时便挪动的胡须，在一角掖进颈间的餐巾上蹭来蹭去。他的小个子太太像一只温厚的鸟，一绺绺泛灰的黑发帘子，从头顶美丽地拧起的发髻两侧，卷曲地垂覆在清朗温柔的前额两边。柔和发亮的眼睛，透过轻巧架在她小巧鼻子上的一副无框夹鼻镜闪着光——没有那种难看的直杠，只有一道小小的半箍把两片镜片连在一起，侧面一条细金链勾过一只耳朵……她的年纪约莫同妈妈在世时相仿……她活脱脱就是她……少女般年轻，却不曾劳忧；那只戴着一枚小白戒指的左手，指尖出奇地会表情，翻回的拇指尖出奇地灵活，就是她本人的缩影。那只手拈着一小块面包，在她喝汤的时候，尖尖地、富于表情地拈着。她熟悉至极，绝非陌生人；是一直相识的。米丽亚姆爱慕着，追着寻她的眼睛，直到她望过来，迎上一阵温柔的、笼罩人的欢迎，那欢迎不打断她绵续的柔和的活泼。唯一奇怪的是她说话时那下颌一种奇妙的*画圆*的摆动；某些安静的词从她嘴里出来时带着一种大张的口形，穿行于、夹杂于、应和着她丈夫那更响亮、清晰可闻的丝绒嗓音。贝利太太坐在那里毫无惧色，在幸福里舒张。您*一定*有不少要看的，她正说着。我们就要指望这位小伙子当我们的向导了，老绅士说着，巨大地转向他更远的邻座。米丽亚姆的眼睛跟过去，撞上赫德医生的脸……*咧着嘴*；他那最浓烈的砖红色的龇笑。他没有走！这就是他的*父母*。*他*也需要假期，这可爱的孩子，老绅士说着，一只手搭上他的肩。赫德医生咧出一个懊丧的否认，眼睛仍望着米丽亚姆的眼睛，说：我不会难过的，脸上皱起他那没爆炸的、歇斯底里跳跃着的笑。赫德太太微笑的小脸掠过一阵迅速压下的伤感。他们千里迢迢从加拿大来分享他的凯旋，却在这里抚平他的败绩。……加拿大的老人。一位*加拿大*女人……那种画圆的下颌动作是加拿大的元音做出来的。它们对女人小巧的嘴比对男人的扰动更大。它一定影响她的思想，那总是张着的嘴；给它们*通风*；把它们弄*圆*，弄成同情的平衡，比口型较窄的英语更容易接着往下说。……坐在您儿子旁边的是一位小提琴家。……啊。我们有望听他一奏了。冈纳先生，小个子，羞怯地微笑着，他旁边是一个*巨大*的女人，一张大大的女学生脸，白皙平直的女学生头发，从宽宽的前额上平掀起一拨，怒成一个尖，愤怒地吃着，两条粗壮的胳膊飞快地动，从缀着廉价奶色蕾丝褶边的套袖里伸出来，那蕾丝不情愿地、被迫地扑打着臂膀。灰黄英俊的丈夫，橄榄色，狂怒，自负，胆汁质的类型，俱乐部气，自以为无所不知，在他不自觉的、乌亮滚圆的头颅顶上一片扁平的无知，两口子都在抢接斯科特和西西与冈纳打趣的零头，努力笑着往里挤，好遮住彼此对彼此的狂怒。穷得没法离得更远，住惯了寄宿公寓的生活，吃得飞快，还张望着添。她有好几个兄弟；一段短的、贵族式的上唇，两个好看而轻蔑的鼻孔，兄弟们在外交界或军队里。桌子这一边有个他们认作异类、正盯着看的人；巴罗太太那边再过去一个高个子男人，一副陌生而*精致*的嗓音，带着游移的、抗议般的抑扬；朝世界上说话，用练熟的、打磨过的游移抑扬，像一块被海磨倦的卵石，一程又一程，无论到哪儿都呈现同样磨损的游移弧线，处处永远是异乡人，永远重新端出那陌生的游移的抑扬；不加分别。桌子那一头不曾察觉赫德一家。它那一群游移在加拿大社会的暖光之外。埃莉诺·迪尔正在它的门口摸索，那副模样惹人怜，精致恳求的头低垂着，婴孩似的眉头若有所思。咱们今晚出去逛逛，好不？米丽亚姆用恋人似的低声咕哝。这是朝加拿大生活敞开的大门口做的一个鬼脸；一个哈丽特式的（à la Harriet）讥诮的踢腿。她的心不管不顾地跳着，围着那桩确定的事：写信、寄信。他若在意，他会懂的。赫德太太是来给她看加拿大社会的，掸去偶然接触的缠结与污渍；把一切摆正。我们当然去。迪尔小姐昂然应着，斥责她的粗俗。如今什么都不要紧了，只消把时间填满。

===Original===
The table was breaking up; the Hurds retiring in a backward-turning group talking to Mrs. Bailey, towards the door. The others were standing about the room. The Hurds had gone. *Oh*-no, *that’s* all right, Mrs. Bailey; *I’ll* be all right. It was the wandering voice.﻿ … It went on, up and down, the most curious different singing tones, the sentences beginning high and dropping low and ending on an even middle tone that sounded as if it were going on. It had a meaning without the meaning of the words. Mrs. Bailey went on with some explanation and again the voice sent out its singing shape; up and down and ending on a waiting tone. Miriam looked at the speaker; a tall grey clad man, a thin pale absentminded face, standing towards Mrs. Bailey, in a drooping lounge, giving her all his attention, several people were drifting out of the room, down-bent towards her small form; Eleanor Dear was waiting, sitting docile, making no suggestion, just right, like a sister; but his eyes never met Mrs. Bailey’s; they were fixed, burning, on something far away; his thoughts were far away, on something that never moved. There was a loud rat-tat on the front door, more than a telegram and less than a caller; a claim, familiar and peremptory. Mrs. Bailey looked sharply up. Sissie was ambling hurriedly out of the room. Oh *dear*, chirruped Eleanor softly, *some*one wants to come in. Well; I’ll say goodnight, said the grey figure and turned easily with a curious waiting halting lounge, exactly like the voice, towards the door. It could stop easily, if anyone were coming in, and wander on again in an unbroken movement. The grey shoulders passing out through the door with the gaslight on them had no look of going out of the room, desolate, they looked *desolate*. The room was almost empty. Mrs. Bailey was listening undisguisedly towards the hall. Sissie came in looking watchfully about. It’s Mr. Rodkin, mother dear she said sullenly. *Rod*kin? *’Im?* gasped Mrs. Bailey, transfigured. Can I come in? asked a deep hollow insinuating voice at the door, how do you do Mrs. Bailey? Mrs. Bailey had flung the door wide and was laughing and shaking hands heartily up and down with a small swarthy black moustached little man with an armful of newspapers and a top hat pushed back on his head. *Well*, he said uncovering a small bony sleek black head and sliding into a chair, his hat sticking out from the hand of the arm clasping the great bundle of newspapers. How *grand* you are. Moy word. What’s the meaning of it? His teeth gleamed brilliantly. He had small high prominent cheekbones, yellow beaten-in temples and a yellow hollow face; yet something almost dimpling about his smile. Aren’t we? chuckled Mrs. Bailey taking his hat. Mr. Rodkin drew his hand over his face, yawning Well I’ve been *every*where since I left; *Mos*cow, *Pe*tersburg, Ba*toom*, Harr-*bin*, *every*where. Moy *wort*. Miss Sissie you are a grown-up grand foine young lady. What is it all about? No joke; *tell* me I say. Mrs. Bailey sat at ease smiling triumphantly. A grand foine dinner.﻿ … Well you wouldn’t have me *starve* my boarduz. *Boarders* murmured Mr. Rodkin, *My* God. He jerked his head back with a laugh and jerked it down again. Well it’s good business anyhow. *Bless* my heart! They talked familiarly on, two tired worn people in a little blaze of mutual congratulation. Mr. Rodkin had come to stay at once without going away. He noticed no one but the Baileys and questioned on and on yawning and laughing with sudden jerks of his head.

===Chinese===
餐桌散了；赫德一家倒退着、成群地同贝利太太说着话，朝门口退去。其余的人散站在屋里。赫德一家走了。*哦*——不，*没*事儿，贝利太太；*我*没事儿的。是那游移的嗓音。……它继续着，高一下低一下，最奇妙的各种歌唱的调子，句子起在高处，落到底，收在一个平平的中音上，听着仿佛还要继续。它有一种意思，不等同于词句的意思。贝利太太继续解释着什么，那嗓音又送出它歌唱的形状；起，落，收在一个等待的音上。米丽亚姆看那说话的人；一个灰衣的高个子男人，一张瘦削苍白、心不在焉的脸，朝贝利太太站着，一副慵懒前倾的姿势，把全副注意力给她，几个人正躬着身子、朝她小小的身形飘出屋去；埃莉诺·迪尔等着，乖顺地坐着，不作提议，恰到好处，像一位姐妹；可他的眼睛从不迎贝利太太的眼睛；它们定定地、灼灼地，燃在远远的什么东西上；他的心思在远处，在一件从不移动的事情上。前门响起响亮的叩门声，比一封电报多、比一位来客少；一种声索，熟悉而专横。贝利太太霍然抬头。西西匆匆蹓出屋去。哦*天*，埃莉诺轻声啁啾，*有人*要进来。那么；我道晚安了，灰衣的身影说着，轻易地一转身，带着一种奇妙的、等待的、迟疑的慵懒步态——同那嗓音一模一样——朝门口去。若有人进来，它可以轻易停住，再以一个不间断的动作继续游移过去。那副灰色的肩膀穿过门口出去，煤气灯照在上面，全没有走出房间的样子，凄凉，它们看着*凄凉*。房间几乎空了。贝利太太毫不掩饰地朝门厅听着。西西走进来，警惕地四下张望。是罗德金先生，妈妈亲爱的。她闷闷地说。*罗德*金？*他*？贝利太太倒抽一口气，容光焕发。我能进来吗？门口一个深沉、空洞、迂回的声音问，您好啊贝利太太？贝利太太已经把门甩开，笑着，同一个小个子黑皮肤、黑八字须的男人热热乎乎上下抖着手，那人抱着一怀报纸，一顶大礼帽推在后脑勺上。*好*啊，他说着摘下小小的、骨感的、油亮的黑头颅，滑进一把椅子，帽子从他抱报纸那条胳膊的手里支出来。您可真*气派*。我的天。这是怎么一回事？他的牙齿闪出亮光。他颧骨小小的、高高的、凸出，两边太阳穴发黄凹陷，一张蜡黄的凹脸；可他笑起来几乎有种现酒窝的意思。可不是嘛？贝利太太咯咯笑着接他的帽子。罗德金先生抹了把脸，打着哈欠说：我走了以后可是*哪儿*都去了；*莫*斯科、*彼*得堡、巴*统*、哈*尔*滨，*哪儿*都去了。我的老天。西西小姐，你出落成大人了，好一位气派的小姐。这到底是怎么回事？可别打哑谜；*说*给我听。贝利太太安坐着，得意地笑。一顿气派的好饭。……唉，您总不能让我饿死我的房客们吧。*房客*。罗德金先生喃喃道，*我的*天。他头往后一仰笑，又猛地落下来。不管怎么说，这是好生意。*老天*爷！他们熟稔地谈下去，两个疲惫潦倒的人，在一小片互相道贺的火光里。罗德金先生一来就没再走。他眼里只有贝利一家，打着哈欠、脑袋猛地一抖一抖地笑着，问个不停。

===Original===
Coming back from sitting flirting with Eleanor at Donizetti’s, Miriam wandered impatiently into the dark dining-room. Eleanor was not her guest. Why didn’t she go up to her room and leave her to the dim street-lit dining-room and the nightly journey up through the darkness to her garret in freedom. Bedtime she hinted irritably, tugging at the tether. Bedtime echoed Eleanor, her smooth humouring nurse’s voice bringing in her world of watchful diplomatic manoeuvring, scattering the waiting population of the familiar dim room. I’m going to bed stated Miriam advancing towards the windows. On the table under the window that was the most brightly lit by the streetlamps was a paper, a pamphlet﻿ … coloured; blue. She took it up. It hung limply in her hand, the paper felt pitted and poor, like very thin blotting paper. *Young Ireland* she read printed in thick heavy black lettering across the top of the page. The words stirred her profoundly, calling to something far away within her, long ago. Underneath the thick words two short columns side by side began immediately. They went on for several pages and were followed by short paragraphs with headings; she pressed close to the lit window, peering; there were blotchy badly printed asterisks between small groups of lines. Heavy black headings further on, like the title, but smaller, and followed by thick exclamation signs. It was a sort of little newspaper, the angry print too heavy for the thin paper. Green. It was green all through﻿ … *Ireland*; home-rule. I *say* she exclaimed eagerly. That was the grey man. *Irish.* That’s all going on still she said solicitously to a large audience. *What* dear asked Eleanor’s figure close to her side. Ireland, breathed Miriam. We’ve got a home-ruler in the house. Look at this; green all through. It’s some propaganda, in London, very angry. I ’ope the home-ruler isn’t green all through chuckled Eleanor smoothly. It’s the wearin’ o’ the green scolded Miriam. The Emerald Isle. We’re so stupid. An Irish girl I knew told me she “just couldn’t bear to face thinking” of the way we treat our children.

===Chinese===
同埃莉诺坐在多尼采蒂餐馆（Donizetti's）里调笑了一晚回来，米丽亚姆不耐烦地游进黑暗的餐室。埃莉诺不是她的客人。她为什么不上楼回自己的房间，把昏暗的、街灯照着的餐室留给她，把夜夜穿越黑暗上阁楼的那段路留给她自由地走。该睡了吧。她烦躁地提点，扯着那根系绳。该睡了。埃莉诺应和着，她那平滑的、哄人的保姆嗓音，带进她那个满是最警觉的周旋的世界，驱散这熟悉的昏暗房间里候着的人群。我要去睡了。米丽亚姆说着，朝窗户走去。窗下那张被街灯照得最亮的桌子上，有一张纸，一本小册子……彩色的；蓝的。她拿起来。它软软地垂在她手里，纸面摸上去坑坑洼洼的、寒素，像很薄的吸墨纸。*青年爱尔兰*（Young Ireland），她读着页首粗重的黑体大字。这两个字深深搅动了她，唤起她体内远远的、很久以前的什么。粗黑字底下，两栏短短的文字并排立刻开始。它们一页页延续，后面跟着一条条带标题的短讯；她凑近亮窗，眯眼细看；一小簇一小簇诗行之间，印着斑驳的、印坏了的星号。再往后的标题也是浓黑，同刊名一样，只是小些，后面跟着粗重的惊叹号。这是种小报，愤怒的铅字对那薄纸来说太重了。绿的。它从头绿到尾……*爱尔兰*；地方自治（home-rule）。我*说*，她急切地喊出来。这就是那个灰衣男人。*爱尔兰人*。这些事还在进行呢。她殷切地对一大群听众说。*什么*，亲爱的？埃莉诺的身影凑在她身边问。爱尔兰。米丽亚姆轻声说。咱们家里住进了一位自治派。瞧这个；从头绿到尾。是某种宣传品，在伦敦，火气很大。我希望这位自治派先生可别从头绿到尾哟。埃莉诺平滑地咯咯笑。这叫“戴绿”（the wearin' o' the green，爱尔兰老歌）呢。米丽亚姆嗔道。翡翠岛（Emerald Isle）。我们多蠢啊。我认识的一个爱尔兰姑娘跟我说，一想到我们对待孩子的那副样子，她“简直没法硬着心思想下去”。

===Original===
Leaving Eleanor abruptly in darkness in her bedroom she shut the door and stepped into freedom. The cistern gurgled from the upper dark freshness. Her world was uninvaded. Klah-rah *Buck*, in reverent unctuousness, waiting for responsive awe from those sitting round. He meant Clara Butt. Then she had been to Canada. He had expected.﻿ … Little Mrs. Hurd had sat birdlike at a Morning Musical hearing the sweep of the tremendous voice. I have never heard it, but I know how it rolls tremendously out and sweeps. I can hear it by its effect on them. They would not believe that. Rounding the sweep of the little staircase she was surprised by a light under the box-room door. Mrs. Bailey, at midnight, busy in the little box-room? How could she find room to have the door shut? Her garret felt fresh and free. Summer rain pattering on the roof in the darkness. The *Colonisation of Ulster*. Her mind turned the pages of a school essay, page after page, no red-ink corrections, the last page galloping along one long sentence; “until England shall have recognised her cruel folly.” 10; *excellent*, E. B. R. A fraud and yet not a fraud. Never having thought of Ireland before reading it up in Green, and then some strange indignation and certainty, coming suddenly while writing; there for always. I had forgotten about it. A man’s throat was cleared in the box-room. The tone of the wandering voice.﻿ … Mrs. Bailey had screwed him into that tiny hole. *I’ll* be all *right*.﻿ … What a shame. He must not know anyone knew he was there. He did not know he was the first to disturb the top landing.﻿ … He did not disturb it. There were no English thoughts in there, nothing of the downstairs house. Julia Doyle, Dublin Bay, *Clontarf*; fury underneath, despairing of understanding, *showing* how the English understood nothing, themselves nor anyone else. But the Irish were not people﻿ … they did not care for *anything*. Meredith was partly Celtic. *That* was why his writing always felt to be pointing in some invisible direction. He wrote so much because he did not care about anything. Novelists were angry men lost in a fog. But how did they find out how to do it? Brain. Frontal development. But it was not certain that that was not just the extra piece wanted to control the bigger muscular system. Sacrificed to muscle. Going about with more muscles and a bit more brain, if *size* means *more*, doing all kinds of different *set* pieces of work in the world, each in a space full of problems none of them could agree about.

===Chinese===
她把埃莉诺骤然撂在黑暗的卧室里，关上门，跨进自由。水箱在上方清凉的黑暗里咕咕作响。她的世界不曾被入侵。克拉-拉·*巴特*（Klah-rah Buck，罗德金把女低音歌唱家克拉拉·巴特 Clara Butt 的名字念走了样），恭恭敬敬、油油腻腻，等着围坐的人报以相应的敬畏。他说的是克拉拉·巴特。那么她是去过加拿大了。他早料到的。……小小的赫德太太曾像只鸟儿坐在一场晨间音乐会（Morning Musical）里，听那洪大嗓音的扫荡。我从没听过它，可我知道它怎样雷霆万钧地滚出来、扫过去。凭它落在他们身上的效果，我听得见它。他们不会信这个。绕上小楼梯的弯时，储物间门下一线灯光让她吃了一惊。贝利太太，半夜里，在那间小储物间里忙碌？她怎么塞得下身、关得上门？她的阁楼清凉而自由。夏雨在黑暗里打在屋顶上。《乌尔斯特的殖民》（The Colonisation of Ulster）。她的头脑翻动一篇学校作文的纸页，一页又一页，没有红笔批改，最后一页一句话长驱直下；“直到英格兰认清她自己残忍的愚行。” 10 分；*优秀*，E. B. R.。一场作假，又不是作假。先前从没想过爱尔兰，读绿皮书读到它，然后某种奇异的义愤和确信，写作时突然降临；从此长在。我都把它忘了。储物间里清了一下男人的嗓子。那游移嗓音的调子。……贝利太太把他拧进了那个小洞里。*我*没*事儿*的。……多可惜。他不能知道有人知道他在那儿。他不知道，他是头一个搅动这处顶层楼梯平台的人。……他没有搅动它。那里面没有英国的思想，没有楼下这幢房子的任何东西。朱莉娅·多伊尔（Julia Doyle），都柏林湾（Dublin Bay），*克隆塔夫*（Clontarf）；底下的狂怒，对理解绝望，*演示*着英国人怎样什么都不懂——不懂他们自己，也不懂任何别人。可爱尔兰人不是人……他们*什么都不*在乎。梅瑞狄斯（Meredith）有一部分凯尔特血统。*所以*他的文字才总觉得指向某个看不见的方向。他写那么多，因为他什么都不在乎。小说家是些迷在雾里的愤怒的人。可他们是怎么发现怎么做到的？脑子。额叶的发达。可也未必不是刚好缺的那一块，好驾驭更庞大的肌肉系统。牺牲给了肌肉。带着更多的肌肉和多一点脑子走来走去——倘若*大*就等于*多*——在世上做着各色各样刻板的活计，每人待在一个问题成堆的空间里，谁跟谁都谈不拢。

---

===Original===
“*Gracious.* You’ll ave to be up early in the morning to say all those names dear.”

===Chinese===
“*天哪。*明儿一早你就得起个大早，才念得完那些名字呢，亲爱的。”

## X / X

===Original===
Eleanor’s cab rumbled away round the corner. Mrs. Bailey was still standing at the top of the steps. Miriam ran up the steps looking busily ahead. It’s going to be a lovely evening she said as she passed Mrs. Bailey. She was safely in the hall. But the front door was closed and Mrs. Bailey was in the hall just behind her. She turned abruptly, almost colliding with her, into the dining-room. Mrs. Bailey’s presence was there waiting for her in the empty room. Behind her just inside the door was Mrs. Bailey, blocking the way to the untrammelled house. There’s quite a lot of August left she quoted from the thoughts that had poured down to meet her as she stood facing the stairs. The clock on the mantelpiece was telling the time of Mrs. Bailey’s day. The empty room was waiting for the next event, a spread meal, voices sounding towards a centre, distracting attention from its increasing shabbiness﻿ … there was never *long* for it to remain sounding its shabbiness, the sound of dust, into the empty space. Events going on and on, giving no time to get in, behind the dusty shabbiness to the sweet dreams and health and quiet breathing.﻿ …

===Chinese===
埃莉诺的马车辘辘地拐过街角去了。贝利太太还站在台阶顶上。米丽亚姆跑上台阶，忙碌地望着前方。今晚准是个可人的夜晚。她经过贝利太太身边时说。她安然进了门厅。可正门关上了，贝利太太就在门厅里，紧跟在她身后。她猛地一转身，差点同她撞个满怀，进了餐室。贝利太太的存在已经先在空房间里等着她了。她身后、门内一步的地方，就是贝利太太，堵住了通往那无拘无束的房子的路。八月还剩好些日子呢。她引用着方才面朝楼梯时涌下来迎她的那些念头。壁炉架上的钟正报着贝利太太一天里的时辰。空房间在等下一桩事，一顿摆开的饭，一阵阵朝着一个中心响去的人声，把注意力从它日甚一日的寒酸上引开……它从来不会有*多久*能把它的寒酸、把尘土的声音，送进那空寂的空间。事情一桩接一桩，不给人时间钻进去——钻到那蒙尘的寒酸背后，去到甜梦、健康和平静的呼吸。……

===Original===
“What a jolly big room this *is*, isn’t it?” she demanded, turning towards Mrs. Bailey’s shapely skimpy form. Mrs. Bailey knew she was chafing in the airless shabby room. The windows closed to keep the dust *out* made the dust *smell*.

“Isn’t it?” agreed Mrs. Bailey cordially.

“You *must* have been glad to get rid of the lodgers and have possession of the whole house.”

“Yes” said Mrs. Bailey straightening the sideboard cloth.

Hearty agreement about the advantages and disadvantages of boarders and then, I think it’s very *plucky* of you and away upstairs. A few words about the interest of having boarders to begin getting to the door with.

“The Irishman’s an interesting specimen of humanity.”

===Chinese===
“这房间可真*够*宽敞的，是不是？”她发问道，转向贝利太太那匀称而瘦小的身形。贝利太太知道她正在这间不透气的寒酸屋子里发闷。窗子关着是为了把灰尘挡在*外*面，反倒让灰尘有了*气味*。

“可不是嘛。”贝利太太热诚地附和。

“摆脱了那些房客、独占整幢房子，您*一定*高兴坏了吧。”

“是啊。”贝利太太说着，理了理餐具柜上的桌布。

先热热乎乎地赞同一番包伙客的种种利弊，然后一句“我看您可真*有胆量*”，再上楼去。谈谈收包伙客的趣味什么的，好歹算是朝门口挪动了。

“那位爱尔兰人是个有趣的人类样本。”

===Original===
“Isn’t he interesting,” laughed Mrs. Bailey moving further into the room.

“It’s much more interesting to have boarders than lodgers,” said Miriam moving along the pathway of freedom towards the open door. Mrs. Bailey stood silent, watching politely. There was no way out. Mrs. Bailey’s presence would be waiting in the hall, and upstairs, unappeased. Miriam glanced towards her without meeting her eyes and sat limply down on the nearest chair.

“Phoo﻿—it’s rather a relief,” she murmured.

Mrs. Bailey went briskly to the door and closed it and came freely back into the room, a little exacting figure who had seen all her selfish rejoicing. She would get up now and walk about the room, talking easily and eloquently about Eleanor’s charm and go away leaving Mrs. Bailey mystified and disposed of.

“My *word*” declared Mrs. Bailey tweaking the window curtains. Then Mrs. Bailey *was* ready and anxious to talk her over and impart her opinion. After seeming to like her so much and being so attentive and sending her off so gaily and kindly, she had some grievance. It was not the bill. It was a matter of opinion. Mrs. Bailey had been charmed and had yet seen through her. Seen what? What was the everlasting secret of Eleanor? She imagined them standing talking together, politely, and joking and laughing. Mrs. Bailey would like Eleanor’s jokes; they would be in agreement with her own opinions about things. But she had formed some idea of her and was ready to express it. If it explained anything one would have to accept it, from Mrs. Bailey. To make nice general remarks about her and enquire insincerely about the bill would be never to get Mrs. Bailey’s uninfluenced opinion. She would not give it unless she were asked.

===Chinese===
“他可有意思了。”贝利太太笑着，往房间深处走。

“收包伙客可比收房客有意思多了。”米丽亚姆说着，沿着那条自由的小径朝敞开的门挪去。贝利太太默默站着，礼貌地看着。没有出路。贝利太太的存在还会在门厅里等着，在楼上等着，不得安抚不肯罢休。米丽亚姆朝她瞥了一眼，避开她的眼睛，软软地在最近的一把椅子上坐了下来。

“呼——总算松了口气。”她喃喃道。

贝利太太利索地走到门口，把门关上，又自如地回到房间里，一个小小的、步步紧逼的身形，把她那点自私的欢庆看了个全。她现在该站起来，在屋里走动，从容流利地谈一阵埃莉诺的魅力，然后走掉，留下贝利太太一头雾水、败下阵去。

“我的*天*。”贝利太太整整窗帘，宣布道。这么说贝利太太*果然*憋着想数落她、想发表她的意见。先前显得那么喜欢她、那么殷勤、那么欢欢喜喜客客气气地送她走，原来心里藏着不满。不是账单的事。是一桩看法。贝利太太被迷住过，却又看穿了她。看穿了什么？埃莉诺那个永远的秘密到底是什么？她想象她们俩站着客客气气地交谈，说笑打趣。贝利太太会喜欢埃莉诺的玩笑；那些玩笑会同她自己对种种事情的看法合拍。可她已经对她形成了某种看法，正准备说出来。倘若那看法能解释什么，人就得接受它，从贝利太太手里接受。只对她作些客气的泛泛之论、再假意问问账单，就永远得不到贝利太太不受影响的意见。不问，她不会说。

===Original===
“I’m awfully sorry for her,” she said in Eve’s voice. That would mean just her poverty and her few clothes and delicate health. There could be an insincere discussion. It might end in nothing and the mean selfish joy would still be waiting upstairs as soon as one had forgotten that it was mean and selfish.

“So am *I*” said Mrs. Bailey heartily. There was anger in her face. There really *was* something, some really bad opinion about Eleanor. Mrs. Bailey thought these things more important than joyful freedom. She was one of those people who would do things; then there were other people too; then one need not trouble about what it was or warn people against Eleanor. The world would find out and protect itself, passing her on. If Mrs. Bailey felt there was something wrong, no one need feel blamed for thinking so. There was. *What* was it?

“I’m the last to be down on anyone in difficulties” said Mrs. Bailey.

===Chinese===
“我真为她难过。”她用伊芙的嗓音说。这只会意味着她的贫穷、她那几件衣裳和她娇弱的身体。可以有一场不真诚的讨论。也许不了了之，而那点卑鄙自私的快乐仍旧在楼上等着，只等人忘掉它卑鄙自私。

“我也*是*。”贝利太太由衷地说。她脸上有怒容。看来*果然*有事，对埃莉诺真有什么坏看法。贝利太太认为这些事比欢快的自由要紧。她是那种会行事的人；何况还有别人；那么人就不必操心那到底是什么，也不必提醒人防着埃莉诺。世界自会察觉、自会自卫，把她一路传下去。既然贝利太太觉得有不对，谁这么想也不必自责。是有不对。*到底*是什么？

“我最不会看低落难的人。”贝利太太说。

===Original===
“Oh yes.” It was coming.

“It’s the *way* of people *I* look to.” She stopped. If she were not pressed she would say no more.

“Oh, by the way, Mrs. Bailey, has her bill been settled?” The voice of Mrs. Lionel﻿ … she’s unsquashable my dear, absolutely unsquashable. You never saw *anything* like it in your *life*. But she’s done frerself in Weston. It might finish the talk.

“That’s all in order young lady. It’s not that at all.”

“Oh, I know. I’m glad though.”

“I had my own suspicions before you told me you’d be responsible. I never thought about that.”

===Chinese===
“哦，是。”来了。

“我看的是人的*做派*。”她停住了。不追问，她就不再说了。

“哦，对了，贝利太太，她的账结清了吗？”莱昂内尔太太（Mrs. Lionel）的嗓音……她呀，压不垮的，亲爱的，绝对压不垮。你这辈子*见都没见过*那样的。可她在韦斯顿（Weston）给自己张罗出局面来了。这也许能把话头收掉。

“都办妥了，小姐。根本不是那码事。”

“哦，我知道。不过我还是高兴。”

“您跟我说您会担保之前，我自己就起过疑心。那件事我压根没想过。”

===Original===
“No, I see.”

“It’s the *way* of people.”

“Well you know I told you at once that you must have her here at your own risk after the first week, and that I hardly knew anything about her.” If she had paid the two weeks so easily perhaps Mr. Taunton was still looking after her needs. No. She would have mentioned him. He had dropped her entirely; after all he had said.

“I’m not blaming you, young lady.” Perhaps Mrs. Bailey had offered advice and been rebuffed in some way. There would be some mysterious description of character; like the Norwegian﻿ … “selfish in a way I couldn’t describe to you.”﻿ …

“If I’d known what it was going to be I’d not have had her in the house two days.”

===Chinese===
“是，我明白。”

“是人的*做派*问题。”

“这个您是知道的，我一开始就跟您说了，过了头一个礼拜，您收留她就得自担风险，再说我对她也几乎一无所知。”两个星期的账她付得那么爽快，说不定汤顿先生（Mr. Taunton）还在照应她的用度。不。不然她会提起他的。他把她整个甩了；说了那么多之后。

“我不是怪您，小姐。”也许贝利太太出过什么主意，被不软不硬地顶了回来。接下来会有某种神秘的性格鉴定；像那次说挪威人……“有种自私，我说不出来给您听。”……

“我要是早知道会是这样，收她进门两天都嫌多。”

===Original===
… some man﻿ … who?﻿ … but they were out all day and Eleanor had been with her every evening. Besides Mrs. Bailey would sympathise with that.﻿ … She was furiously angry; “not two days.” But she *had* been charmed. Charmed and admiring.

“Did she flirt with someone?”

“That” said Mrs. Bailey gravely, “I can’t tell you. She may have; that’s her own affair. I wouldn’t necessary blame her. Everyone’s free to do as they like provided they behave theirselves.” Mrs. Bailey was brushing at her skirt with downcast eyes.

… This woman had opened Dr. von Heber’s letter; knew he was coming next year; knew that he “would not have permitted” any talk at all, and that all her interference was meaningless. *He* was coming, carrying his suitcase out of the hospital, no need for the smart educated Canadian nurses to think about him﻿ … taking ship﻿ … coming back. Perhaps she resented having been in the wrong.

===Chinese===
……某个男人……谁？……可他们白天整天不在，埃莉诺每个傍晚都同她在一起。再说，这一层贝利太太只会同情。……她怒不可遏；“两天都嫌多”。可她*确实*被迷住过。又迷又叹。

“她是不是同什么人调情了？”

“那个嘛，”贝利太太郑重地说，“我没法告诉您。也许有；那是她自己的事。我也不见得就怪她。谁都有自由爱怎么做就怎么做，只要举止规矩。”贝利太太垂着眼，掸她的裙子。

……这个女人拆看过冯·黑贝尔医生的信；知道他明年要来；知道他“根本不会容许”任何闲话，知道她这一切多管闲术全都毫无意义。*他*要来的，拎着提箱走出医院，用不着那些漂亮、受过教育的加拿大护士为他操心……登船……回来。也许她怨的是自己当初错了。

===Original===
“It was funny how she found a case so suddenly,” said Miriam drawing herself upright, careless, like a tree in the wind. She had already forgotten she would always feel like that, her bearing altered forever, held up by him, like a tree in the wind, everyone powerless to embarrass her. Poor Mrs. Bailey.﻿ …

“You see I feel I drove her to it, in a way.”

Mrs. Bailey listened smiling keenly.

“Yes you *see*” pursued Miriam cheerfully, “I told her she would be all right for a week. I blamed *you* for that, said you were flourishing and she could pay when her ship came home.”

“That’s what you told her eh?”

===Chinese===
“真有意思，她那么突然就找到了一个看护的位子。”米丽亚姆说着，把身子挺直，无所顾忌，像风里的一棵树。她已经忘了自己从此都会这样感觉，她的仪态永远变了，被他扶着，像风里的一棵树，谁也没法再让她难堪。可怜的贝利太太。……

“您瞧，某种意义上，我觉得是我把她逼到那一步的。”

贝利太太听着，热切地微笑。

“是吧，您*瞧*。”米丽亚姆快活地接着说，“我跟她说，一个星期总不成问题。这一点我怪的是*您*，说您生意红火，等她时来运转再付不迟。”

“您是这么跟她说的，嗯？”

===Original===
“Well and then when she admitted she had no money and I knew I couldn’t manage more than a week, I advised her to apply to the C.O.S. She said she would and seemed delighted and when I asked her about it later she cried and said she hadn’t been. I said she must do *something* and then suddenly this case appeared. *Where* I don’t know.”

“I don’t blame her for not wanting to go *there*.”

“Why?”

“My word. I’d as soon go straight to the parish.”

“Wilberforce believes in them. He says if you really want to help the helpless you will not flaunt your name in subscription lists but hand your money over to the C.O.S. They are the only charitable organization that does not pauperise.”

===Chinese===
“后来她承认自己身无分文，我知道我撑不过一个礼拜，就劝她去求助慈善组织协会（C.O.S.）。她说好，还显得满心欢喜，等我过后问起，她哭了，说一直没去。我说她总得*做点什么*，然后这个看护的位子就突然冒出来了。*哪儿*来的，我不知道。”

“她不肯上*那儿*去，我不怪她。”

“为什么？”

“我的天。我还不如直接去求教区救济呢。”

“威尔伯福斯（Wilberforce）信得过它们。他说，你要是真想帮那些无助的人，就不该把名字张扬在捐款名单上，而该把钱交到慈善组织协会手里。它们是唯一不把人养成贫民的慈善组织。”

===Original===
“Him? Wilberforce? He has a right to his own opinions I don’t deny. But if he’d ever been in difficulties he might change them. *Insulting*, that’s my opinion. My word the *questions* they ask. You can’t call your soul your own.”

“I didn’t know that. That friend my sister brought here was being helped by them.”

“How is Miss Henderson?”

“Perfectly happy. Being with the Greens again seems *Paradise* she says, after London. She’s satisfied now.”

“Mts. She’s a sweet young lady; them’s fortunate as have her.”

===Chinese===
“他？威尔伯福斯？他有权利坚持自己的见解，这我不否认。可他要是落过一回难，说不定就改了。*侮辱人*，这是我的看法。我的天，他们问的那些*问题*。你连自己的灵魂都做不了主。”

“这我不知道。我姐姐带来的那位朋友，就正受着他们的资助。”

“亨德森小姐好吗？”

“幸福极了。她说回到格林家（the Greens）身边，简直是*天堂*，比起伦敦来。她如今知足了。”

“啧啧。她是位甜心的小姐；谁有她谁福气。”

===Original===
“Well now she’s tried something else she appreciates the beautiful home. I don’t think she wants to be free.”

“Quite so. Persons differ. But she’s her own mistress; free to leave.”

“Of course it’s nicer now. The children are at school. She’s confidential companion. They all like her so much. They invented it for her.”

“Quite right. That’s as it should be.”

“And she is absolutely in Mrs. Green’s confidence now. I don’t know what poor Mrs. Green would do without her. She went back just in time for a most *fearful* tragedy.”

===Chinese===
“如今她试过了别的路，才懂得欣赏那个美好的家。我看她不想自由了。”

“正是。人各有异。不过她是自己的主人；来去自由。”

“现在自然更舒心了。孩子们都上了学。她做的是贴心女伴。他们都那么喜欢她。这职位是他们为她特设的。”

“这就对了。理当如此。”

“如今格林太太（Mrs. Green）的心事她全知道。没有她，可怜的格林太太可怎么办，我不知道。她回去得正是时候，赶上了一场*骇人*的悲剧。”

===Original===
“Tss; dear﻿—*dear*” murmured Mrs. Bailey waiting with frowning calm eagerness. Miriam hesitated. It would be a long difficult story to make Mrs. Bailey see stupid commercial wealth. She would see wealthy “people,” a “gentleman” living in a large country house, and not understand Mr. Green at all; but *Eve*, getting the bunch of keys from the ironmonger’s and writing to Bennett to find out about Rupert Street﻿ … and the *detective*. She would have it in her mind like a novel and never let it go. It would be a breach of confidence.﻿ … She paused, not knowing what to do with her sudden animation. It was too late to get back into being an impartial listener, on the verge of going away. She had told everything, without the interesting details. Mrs. Bailey was waiting for them. They were still safe. She might think it was an illness or something about a relative. The only thing to do now was to stay and work off the unexplained animation on anything Mrs. Bailey might choose to say. “Well” said Mrs. Bailey presently, “to return to our friend. What I say is, why doesn’t she go to the clergy, in her own parish?”

“Go on the parish, m’m.”

“Not necessarily on the parish. The clergy’s most helpful and sympathetic. They might tell her of those who would help her.”

“They might. But it’s most awfully difficult. *Nobody* knows what ought to be done about these things.”

===Chinese===
“啧；哎呀——*哎呀*。”贝利太太喃喃着，皱着眉，平静而急切地等着。米丽亚姆犹豫了。要让贝利太太看明白愚蠢的商业财富，得讲一个又长又难的故事。她只会看见有钱的“人家”、住在大乡宅里的“绅士”，根本理解不了格林先生；而*伊芙*——从五金店取回那串钥匙，写信给贝内特（Bennett）打听鲁珀特街（Rupert Street）……还有那名*侦探*。她会把它像一部小说一样收进脑子里，再也放不掉。那会是一种泄密。……她顿住了，不知拿这阵突发的兴奋怎么办。再想退回那个不动声色的听客身份、那个即将远去的人的身份，已经太迟。她什么都讲了，只除掉有趣的细节。贝利太太正等着那些细节。它们还安全。她也许会当成一场病，或者哪个亲戚的事。眼下唯一的办法是留下来，把这份没来由的兴奋消磨在贝利太太肯说的任何事情上。“这个嘛，”贝利太太过了一会儿说，“回到咱们那位朋友。要我说，她干吗不去找她自己教区的牧师？”

“上教区救济，夫人。”

“不一定上教区救济。牧师们顶帮忙、顶热心。他们也许能告诉她谁愿意帮她。”

“也许吧。可这难极了。*谁*都不知道这种事该怎么办。”

===Original===
“That is so. But there’s a right and a wrong in everything. There’s plenty of people willing to help those that will help theirselves. But that’s very different to coming into a person’s house to try and get money out of strangers.”

“I *say*.”

“It is I *say*. I never felt so ashamed in my life.”

“I *say*.﻿ … Did they tell you?”

“Mrs. Hurd came to me herself.”

“Mrs. Hurd. Of course, it would be.”

“My word. I *was* wild. And them only just come into my house.”

===Chinese===
“这话不假。可万事都有个是非。愿意帮那些肯自助者的人有的是。可那同闯进人家的房子、想从陌生人身上抠钱，完全是两码事。”

“我的天。”

“是*我说*的。我这辈子没这么丢过人。”

“我的天。……她们告诉您了？”

“赫德太太亲自来找我的。”

“赫德太太。当然，肯定是。”

“我的天。我*真是*气坏了。他们才进我这房子多久。”

===Original===
“Yes, of course; I *say*.”

“Tellin’ them she was *ill*.”

“She is ill you know.”

“There’s some imagines theirselves ill. If she was anything like as ill as I am she might have something to complain about.”

“I think she’s rather plucky. She doesn’t want to give in. It’s a kind of illness that doesn’t show much. I know her doctor. He’s a Harley Street man. *He* says that her kind of disorder makes it absolutely impossible for the patient to tell the truth. I don’t believe that. It’s just one of those doctory things they all repeat.”﻿ … What is truth said jesting Pilate and did not wait for an answer. *Their* idea of truth﻿—

“Well if she is ill why doesn’t she act according?”

===Chinese===
“是，当然；我的天。”

“还跟人家说她*病*了。”

“她是病了，您知道的。”

“有的人是自己想象出病来。她要是病得有我一半，倒还值得抱怨抱怨。”

“我看她相当有勇气。她不肯认输。她那种病不太看得出来。我认识她的医生。哈利街（Harley Street）的。*他*说，她那一类的毛病，让病人完全不可能说真话。我不信。那不过是医生们人云亦云的一路腔调。”……何谓真理，戏言的彼拉多问道，并不等待回答（What is truth said jesting Pilate）。*他们*的真理观——

“那么她既是病着，干吗不照病人的样子行事？”

===Original===
“Look *after* herself a bit. Yes. That’s what she wants to do. But not give in.”

“Quite so. That’s a thing a person can understand. But that doesn’t make it right to come to private people and behave in the way she has done. Strangers. I never met such conduct, nor heard of it.”

“No.”

“She’s got relatives I suppose; or friends.”

“Well, that’s just it. I don’t think she has. I suppose the truth is all her friends are tired of helping her.”

“Well, I’m not judging her there. There’s none can be so cruel as relatives, as *I* know, my word.”

===Chinese===
“稍稍*将养*自己一下。是啊。她正是想这样。可又不肯认输。”

“正是。这样的事人还理解得来。可这不能让她的行径变得正当——找上私人家里，做出她做下的那副样子。对着陌生人。这样的行径我没见过，也没听说过。”

“是。”

“她总有亲戚吧；或者朋友。”

“唉，问题就在这儿。我看她没有。我猜实情是，她的朋友全都帮她帮累了。”

“唉，这一层我不评断她。谁也没有亲戚狠，这个*我*可是知道的，我的天。”

===Original===
“Yes.”

“They’ll turn from you when you’re struggling to the utmost to help yourself, going on ill, left with four young children, your husband cut off and not a *penny*.”

“Yes.”

“I agree with her there. I owe all I have, under Providence, to my own hands and the help coming from strangers I had no claim on. But why doesn’t she act open? That’s what *I* say and I know it. There’s always those ready to help you if you’ll do your part. It’s all take and no give with some.”

“Vampires. People *are* extraordinary.”

===Chinese===
“是。”

“你拼尽全力自助的时候，他们会掉头而去——病着拖日子，撇下四个年幼的孩子，丈夫没了，一个*子儿*也不剩。”

“是。”

“这一层我倒赞同她。我如今所有的一切，托上天的福，全亏我自己的这两只手，和陌生人给的、我本来无权领受的帮衬。可她干吗不敞亮行事？这是*我*要说的，我清楚。只要你尽你的本分，总有人乐意帮你。有些人哪，光取不予。”

“吸血鬼。人*真是*不可思议。”

===Original===
“You’d say so if you had this house to manage.”

“I suppose so.”

“You get your eyes open. With one and another.”

“I’d no idea she’d even been talking to the Hurds.”

“Talk? Well I don’t mind telling you now she’s gone.”

“Well, she won’t come back again. If she ever does Mrs. Bailey I hereby refuse all responsibility. On your head be it if you take her in. *I* can’t keep her.”

“Well, as I say, I’m free to tell you. They used to go upstairs into the drawn-room, mornings, after breakfast. I could hear that woman’s voice going on and on. I was up and down the stairs. What’s more she used to stop dead the minute I came in.”

===Chinese===
“您要是得打理这么一幢房子，您也会这么说。”

“我想也是。”

“眼睛就睁开了。这个那个的。”

“我压根不知道她同赫德一家还说过话。”

“说过话？唉，她人都走了，我不妨跟您说了吧。”

“唉，她不会再回来了。她真要是回来，贝利太太，我在此声明概不负责。您再收留她，后果您自家担待。*我*可养不起她。”

“唉，我说了，我不妨告诉您。她们从前总在早饭后的上午，上楼进客厅。我听得见那个女人的声音说呀说呀说个不停。我在楼梯上上上下下。更有甚者，我一进去，她准死死地停住。”

===Original===
“Well I am sorry you’ve had all this.”

“I’m not blaming you, young lady.”

“What about all the others?”

“Rodkin and Helsing and Gunner’s out all day.”

“Yes but the others? The Manns and the Irish journalist.”

“She’d be clever to get anything out of any of *them*.”

“I wonder she didn’t try Mrs. Barrow. She’s kind I’m sure and gullible.”

“She’s very kind no doubt in her way. Anyway she’s not one of those who live on a widow woman and pay nothing.”

===Chinese===
“唉，这些事都让您受了。”

“我不怪您，小姐。”

“其余的人呢？”

“罗德金和赫尔辛和冈纳整天都在外头。”

“是，可其余的人呢？曼恩一家，还有那位爱尔兰记者。”

“她要是能从*他们*谁身上掏出点什么，那才叫有本事。”

“奇怪她没去试试巴罗太太。她人好，我知道，又好骗。”

“她心肠好，这没问题，用她那种方式。反正她不是那种寄食在寡妇身上、一个子儿不付的人。”

===Original===
The old sense of the house was crumbling. To Mrs. Bailey it was *worry* and things she could not talk about to anyone, and a few nice people here and there. And all the time she was *polite*; as if she liked them all, equally. And they were polite. Everyone was polite. And behind it was all this. Shifts and secrets and strange characters. When they were all together at Mrs. Bailey’s dinner, they were all carrying things off, politely. Perhaps already she regretted having sent away the lodgers.

“The doctors were nice people to have in the house.”

“Wasn’t they dear boys? *Very* nice gentlemen. Canadians are the ones to my mind, though I believe as much as any in standing by your own. But you’ve got to consider your interests.”

“Of course.”

===Chinese===
这幢房子旧日的感觉正在碎裂。对贝利太太来说，它是*操劳*，是些她无法对任何人讲的事，加上东一个西一个的好人。而她自始至终*客气*着；仿佛她喜欢他们全体，一视同仁。他们也客气。人人都客气。而客气后面全是这些。权宜与秘密与古怪的人物。那晚大家在贝利太太的餐桌旁聚齐时，人人都体面地遮掩过关。也许她已经在后悔撵走了那些房客。

“医生们是住在家里很舒心的人。”

“他们可不就是好孩子？*顶顶*体面的绅士们。照我看，还得数加拿大人，虽说对于自家人要抱团，我信得不比谁少。可人总得考虑自己的利益。”

“当然。”

===Original===
“That’s why I mean to ad*ver*tiss. My word those Hurds are good friends if you like. I couldn’t tell you. The old man’s put an advert for me in the Canadian place in the city.”

“Then you’ll have a houseful of Canadians.”

“That’s what I *hope*. The more the better of their kind.”

“We shall all be speaking Canadian.”

“Well, since we’re on the subject, Mrs. Hurd advises me to *go* to Canada. Says it’s all work and no pay over here. Everybody expects too much for too little.”

How *could* she rejoice in the idea of a house full of Canadians? All the same. Canadian. It would change the house more and more. Mrs. Bailey would not mind that. The house meant nothing to her just as it was with its effect. She had to make it pay. If another house would pay better she would just as soon have another house. “You wouldn’t like to leave London; there’s no place like London.” The Hurds thought everyone in the house *selfish*, living on Mrs. Bailey’s work, enjoying the house for nothing, forgetting her. It was true﻿ … uneasy in her presence.﻿ …

===Chinese===
“所以我打算去登广*告*。我的天，赫德一家可真是好朋友，没说的。我都说不好。老先生已经帮我在城里加拿大人那边登了一则广告。”

“那您就要有一屋子加拿大人了。”

“我*盼*的就是这个。他们那种人，越多越好。”

“咱们都要说加拿大话了。”

“既然说到这个，赫德太太劝我干脆*移民*去加拿大。说这边全是干活没进项。人人都想少出力多要钱。”

一屋子加拿大人的前景，她*怎么能*为之欢欣？可话说回来。加拿大。它会把这房子越变越野。贝利太太不会在意。这房子连同它的气韵，就现在这样，对她毫无意义。她必须让它生利。要是另一幢房子生利更多，她马上就可以换一幢。“您不会想离开伦敦的吧；哪儿也比不上伦敦。”赫德一家觉得屋里所有的人都*自私*，寄在贝利太太的操劳上，白享这幢房子，把她忘在脑后。这是真的……在她面前坐立不安。……

## XI / XI

===Original===
Miriam got up early the next morning and went to her window in her nightgown. There was a thick August haze in the square. The air smelt moist. She leaned out into the chill of it. Her body was full of sleep and strength; all one strength from head to feet. She heard life in the silence, and went through her getting up as quickly as possible, listening all the time to the fresh silence.

She went downstairs feeling like a balloon on a string; her feet touching the stairs lightly as if there were no weight in her body. At the end of the long journey came the smiling familiar surprise of the hall. The hall table was clear, a stretch of grey marble in the morning light. The letters had been taken into the dining-room. There was something, a package, on the far corner, a book package, with a note, Silurian blue, *Eleanor*. Small straggly round handwriting, yes, Eleanor’s, *R. Rodkin, Esq*: *Ah.* Mr. *Rodkin*. How had she done it? When? Carrying off a book. Pretending she had forgotten, and writing. Sly cleverness. What a blessing she had gone.﻿ … Booming through her uneasiness came a great voice from the dining-room. Through the misty corridors of the *Dawn* it bellowed. She went gladly in towards poetry. Mrs. Bailey was presiding over an early breakfast. The Irishman, sitting back mirthfully in his chair on the far side of the table and at his side a big stout man with a bushy black beard, brilliant laughing eyes staring at nothing from a flushed face. Mrs. Bailey was watching him with a polite smile; he looked as though he were at supper; making the room seem hot, obliterating the time of day. I expect you had a rough crossing, she said politely. I *saw* her, he bellowed flinging back his head and roaring out words and laughter together. She walks in *Beauty*. I saw her sandalled feet; upon the *Hills*.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆第二天一早醒来，穿着睡衣走到窗前。广场上罩着厚厚的八月的雾霭。空气闻着潮润。她探身进那一片凉意里。她的身体盛满睡眠和力气；从头到脚浑然一股劲。她在寂静里听见生活，于是尽快完成起床的一切，一路听着那新鲜的寂静。

她下楼去，感觉自己像一只系着线的气球；双脚轻触楼梯，仿佛身体里没有重量。长途旅程的尽头，是门厅那含笑的、熟悉的意外。门厅的桌子空着，一片灰色大理石延展在晨光里。信件已经拿进餐室去了。远角上有什么东西，一个包裹，一个书裹，附着一封短信，《志留纪之蓝》（Silurian blue），*埃莉诺*。歪歪扭扭的圆圆的小字，没错，埃莉诺的，*R. Rodkin, Esq.*（罗德金先生台启）：*啊。*罗德金*先生*。她是怎么做到的？什么时候？把一本书半路截走。装作忘了，然后再写信。狡黠的小聪明。她走了真是万幸。……一阵洪大的嗓音从餐室里隆隆穿透她的不安。它隔着《黎明报》（The Dawn）雾蒙蒙的走廊咆哮。她欣然朝诗歌走去。贝利太太正主持着一顿早早餐。爱尔兰人坐在桌子那头自己的椅子里，快活得后仰着，他旁边一个大个子壮实的男人，一部浓密的络腮黑须，一双灼灼的笑眼从涨红的脸上瞪着虚空。贝利太太带着礼貌的微笑看他；他看上去像在吃一顿夜宵；把房间弄得很热，抹掉了时辰。我想您渡海时风浪不小吧。她客气地说。我*见*到她了。他昂着头咆哮，词句和笑声一齐轰出来。她在*美*中行走（She walks in Beauty，拜伦诗句）。我看见她穿凉鞋的脚；在*群山*之上。
